PONCE MARAICHER OUVRAGE ORNÉ DE 16 PLANCHES LITHOGRAPHIQUES PARIS RAIRIE AGRICOLE DE LA MAISON RUSTIQUE 26, RUE JACOE, 26 1868 All rights reserved. 4 Any copy not bearing this mark will be considered counterfeit. Cucar. - Impr. M. Lerenor, Paul Deroxr et Cie, rut da Bac-d'Asnières, 12. For fifteen years I have been cultivating the land, and for fifteen years I have suffered from the absence of a special treatise which I could consult without fail and which would provide me with positive information and foolproof instructions. This treatise is intended to fill the gap. It is the fruit of long experience and can be consulted with profit. May it spare young people hardship and loss of money, may it help the memory of older workers, may it provide men of fortune with the means to take an interest in the land and receive from it all that it can give, may it inspire all friends of our beautiful soil of France with the taste and the will to increase its prosperity and wealth. And he will have obtained the fortune I dreamed of for him, and which all my efforts have tended to secure for him. Paris, 1869. 1. PONCE. CHAPTER ONE GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS To produce a lot in a small space, to provide vegetables to feed a thousand people by cultivating a plot of land whose surface area would not feed fifty if ordinary processes were applied, and if art did not know how to help nature, such is the problem posed every day to market gardening in the Paris area, And every day, the problem has its solution, although the difficulties accumulate as the vast city crosses its boundaries, erecting new shelters to house new guests, covering the formerly cultivated soil with buildings and forcing those who feed it to seek more sun and space. The horticulturalist occupies a plot of land rarely less than 7,000 meters deep and rarely more than 10,000 meters, all in one piece. They rarely own the land themselves, and pay a higher rent the closer they are to Les Halles, and therefore to the center of their business. However, it takes exceptional circumstances or a poor understanding of his profession for him not to achieve, after a certain number of years' work, a modest ease and, for intelligent people, a justly deserved and always legitimately earned fortune. Competition is a useful source of emulation among market gardeners. Vegetables sold early are excellent value; in season, they provide the grower with enough to live on and pay his expenses; late, they provide a new harvest that is productive in terms of ecus. The soil lends itself admirably to this combination of the demands of human appetite. Siliceous soils, sufficiently fertilized and mixed with a skilfully combined loam, produce early crops. Clay soils, under the same conditions, only produce late; but when the siliceous soil has produced its early crops early, and can no longer retain the moisture needed by food plants, the clay soil will begin to produce its own, and will give late crops in the middle of the hottest weather, because of its property of retaining water like a sponge and delaying the decomposition of fertilizers. The smallest plot in a market garden should not be left unoccupied for more than twenty-four hours. Any plant uprooted must be replaced immediately if the soil does not require a new way; where no plants are to be seen, the worker must be seen at work. As market gardens are always close to housing and population centers, they do not lack fertilizer, but fertilizer is insufficient to achieve the goal of this type of farming, as nature must be forced to provide spring in the middle of winter, and summer during the still short and uncertain days of spring. The soil of the market garden has no rest; although it nourishes the most exhausting plants, its master returns to it, as and when it loses, the elements it needs to comfort itself and provide vegetation with new production. Manure provides the heat, nitrogen, carbon, hydrogen, sulfur, phosphorus and calcium salts it needs. Wooden boxes covered with glass frames protect the young plants from contact with the cold air during the harsh season. A warm, humid temperature, favorable to vegetation, then reigns under these chests, as the air, heated internally by the heat of the sun and the fermentation of manure, does not cool down, experience having shown that heat does pass through the glass in the company of light, but that heat alone can no longer pass through it and remains trapped. In addition to chests, glass cloches are used to retain the heat of the sun's rays. The shape of the market garden is usually that of a more or less elongated rectangle; this is the shape that should be sought, as it is easier to supervise and divide up. The plot must be fenced off, both against marauders, animals and people, and against the wind. The best fence is a good wall; but if you can't use one, at least fence with a good chestnut trellis, against which you can apply straw mats, called windbreaks. The land should be divided into rectangles, with the longest dimension facing south. These rectangles should be set square, and those intended for frames should have a dimension of 21 meters to accommodate 15 frames, i.e. 20 meters for the frames and one meter for a pathway and two supports. They are laid out end-to-end in this way, throughout the southern exposure. When it becomes impossible to obtain a new 21-meter line, transverse lines formed in the same way are laid out at sunset. This is called making squares. A 2.34-metre rectangular board that runs the full length of the wall or windbreak established on the south side is known as the "costière"; this board is separated from a second one by a 33-centimetre path. This second board is called the counter-costiere. A new path separates the counter-costiere from the space reserved for the frames or the square. The other boards after the square are laid out in the same way as the upstand and counter-upper. The border and counter-border are repeated on the opposite side of the garden; only the second counter-border is divided into paths every 20 meters for watering. As far as possible, the grower's dwelling should be on the roadside and at the southern corner. It generally consists of a basement used as a store, a first floor and a second floor; next to this is the stable, the shed and the hangar for equipment; in front is an open space containing the well, the manege or the steam pump, the reservoir, if there is one, the tank for washing produce for sale, and the manure dump. Abundant water at one's discretion is the essential condition for market gardening. Prior to 1859, all such gardens were watered using the following system. A fulling pump driven by a manege lifted the water above ground, through a pipe, into a tank that fed a system of barrels buried at ground level, connected by terracotta tubes anointed with bitumen. These tubes do not lead directly to the barrel; a lead pipe, fitted with a stopper or swan-necked tap, runs from the buried pipe at a distance of at least 40 centimetres or 50 centimetres at most, and empties its contents into the barrel. Once the barrel is full, the pipe is plugged with the cork or tap. In some operations, the barrels are connected to each other by the pipe, so that the whole system fills up successively; this is a bad method, as the last barrel can only be filled when the others are full, whereas the system described allows water to be supplied to one, several or all the barrels at once, as desired. Buried barrels hold 2 hectoliters or more, are strapped in iron, and tarred on the outside to prevent rotting. They are placed 10 meters 66 apart, i.e. every four planks. The worker draws water from them using two copper watering cans. The author of this book has adopted, and had many of his colleagues adopt, a less primitive and much more advantageous watering system, the layout of which is his own invention. The barbaric barrel system takes a considerable amount of space away from production, and the men who do the watering are barefoot in the sodden earth all day long. Their work is very time-consuming, and is perhaps the most arduous of their profession: how many pleurisies, chest fluxions and rheumatisms have been contracted in such circumstances! - Barrels on the ground, at the crossroads of trails, have been the cause of deplorable accidents. There are hardly any years in which the newspapers don't record a few deaths of children or old people drowned in market-garden barrels, some as a result of the carelessness inherent in their age, others as a result of failing eyesight. All human motives: enjoyment, because the land watered by the new system no longer presents the muddy, odorous cesspools found around every barrel of the old system; 1. interest, because land is gained and labor saved; and finally, duty, because all danger of disease and death is eliminated for one's own and for others; all human motives, we say, must urge market gardeners to adopt the new watering system. In this system, which we shall describe in detail later, the water from the food pump, raised above the ground to a height of 5 to 6 meters, is poured into a sheet metal tank of 20 to 22 cubic meters capacity, more or less. The terracotta pipe is replaced by sandstone pipes solidly joined together by a broken ring embedded in cement, or by cast-iron or lead pipes. Barrels are eliminated. In their place, however, there is a water intake to which a 15-meter-long rubber tube is connected, fitted with a hose and a watering can head. The expensive taps of ordinary water intakes are replaced by a tubular piece of rubber, clamped to the cast-iron intake by means of a collar and closed when not in use by means of a pressure screw. The connection to the sprinkler pipe is made by means of a sleeve clamped by the elasticity of the rubber. Thanks to this system, the worker can water with dry feet, without getting wet or bending over; the lack of fatigue attaches him to this type of work, the most essential to his profession, and which was formerly performed only with bad humor due to the fatigue and dangers to which the worker was exposed. The market gardener cultivates all the food plants that grow naturally in our climate; however, if he confined himself to those that are easy to grow and require the same amount of time to develop as field crops, he wouldn't be spending his money. This is why he limits himself to plants that can be cultivated relatively quickly, and whose normal growth time he reduces by using the instruments at his disposal to obtain the temperature and humidity levels he requires. He also cultivates certain annual plants from warmer climates, which are much sought-after by Parisian appetites, and which acquire finer and more delicate qualities through this artificial cultivation. The honorable profession of market gardener counts only honest, hard-working people among its members. All of them, with the two indispensable aids of health and drive, achieve their goals. Women are in charge of sales and harvesting: women's activity is one of the first conditions for success. All the market gardener's commercial operations are cash-based. What he hasn't been able to sell during the day on the Halles square is put into the greenhouse and put back on sale the next day. In general, he has to sell at the day's price; this can sometimes be a disappointment, but the stale harvest the next day will rarely be worth more than the day before, so it's with regret that he turns to his Paris greenhouse. As we said at the start of this chapter, he has two problems to solve: arriving first for a certain product, or arriving last; in both cases there's a business to be done. The interval is just a routine trade that still pays its way, and is by no means to be scorned. A great deal of progress has been made over the last few years, and many new crops have been introduced, especially since Les Halles opened its doors to goods from the south of France brought to Paris by the railroads, which had to compete with them. This is a warning to market gardeners to try to be one step ahead and do better; the interest and honor of the trade oblige them to do so, and they will not fail. COMPOSITION OF A MARAICHER'S GARDEN We think it useful, for the intelligence of the work and the good understanding of the layout of a market gardener's garden, to describe one, such as it exists: it is that of the author of this book, who has endeavored to have an establishment without rival in the vicinity of Paris and who, self-love aside, believes he has succeeded. The cultivated surface area is 11,000 square meters. Watering is by hose, according to an economical system invented by the author of this book. The equipment includes a two-horsepower steam engine for the fulling pump when the horse is occupied, and a manege for the same service when the horse is available. - The whole is worth 1,700 francs for the pump, and 2,000 francs for the machine. The water is raised, by means of a lead pipe, to the upper part of a bolted sheet metal tank, with a capacity of 22 cubic meters. This tank is placed on a platform, 5.60 meters above ground level. A massive frame and masonry supports the whole. - The whole cost 1,300 francs. Two large lead pipes carry the water down to sandstone pipes buried 40 centimetres below ground. In these pipes, the water is subjected to a pressure that varies between one atmosphere when the tank is full and one third atmospense is 88 francs. The water mains are made up of 550 meters of sandstone pipe, to which 70 water intakes are fitted for watering purposes. Watering is by means of two rubber tubes, each 14 meters long, onto which a copper lance is mounted, ending in a watering can head. - Expenditure: 1,582 francs for the pipe, and 260 francs for the rubbers and their lance. 950 frames and their boxes, and 3,500 bells are used for the cultivation of early fruit and vegetables. 800 straw mats are used to cover the frames in times of frost during the night, and to protect the plants from the effects of the cold. - Total cost: 15,700 francs. 4 spades, 2 iron rakes, 4 iron shovels, 2 rakes, 4 planters, 2 ropes, 2 squares: this is the plowing equipment. - Value: 60 francs. Two stretchers with their two pairs of odds and ends are used to transport the frames, bells and crops. - Total cost: 48 francs. Transporting crop manure also requires 4 loaders, 4 hoods, an unlimited supply of mannes, mannettes and mannequins, and a large wheelbarrow. - Total cost: 175 francs. Manure work requires 6 forks and 2 hooks. - Together, 32 francs. Tomatoes require 1,000 stakes and 30 rolls of wire. - Cost: 250 francs. A gardener's cart and a horse are used to transport crops for sale. - Total, including harness, 1,730 francs. In addition to the head of the establishment and the lady of the house, the staff consists of three servants, one of whom is in charge of taking the crops for sale to the Halles and bringing manure back to the farm; the rest of his time is taken up with gardening work, while his two colleagues devote all their time to it. - Annual expenditure is 1,620 francs. Two daughters, one of whom devotes her time half to sales, half to harvesting, and a day laborer during the summer, complete the staff. They are paid 600 francs a year. Using the above equipment and personnel, we produce the following per year: 24 hot layers, producing carrots, black lettuce and spinach. 12 hot-air layers, producing radishes. 40 cold beds, where parsley, sorrel, parsnips, various lettuces and cauliflower are harvested. 36 banks of romaine, some under cloches, others cold grown with carrots. Melons and tomatoes require 70 to 80 trenches, where the plants are placed under cloches, and a few trenches for early green beans. Depending on the season, the rest of the land is planted with cauliflower, apple cabbage, onions, spring onions, civet, parsley, chervil, sorrel, pink and black radishes, leeks, carrots, romaine, lettuce and spinach, all grown outdoors and cold. An establishment set up in this way provides the following average harvests per year: During the spring season: 10,800 feet of black lettuce, hot-grown. 240 bunches of spinach, obtained in the same way; average weight per bunch: 1,500 gr. 1,600 bunches of carrots, obtained in the same way; average weight per bunch: 2 kilograms. 1,000 bunches of radishes, obtained both hot and cold. 11,000 feet of Georges lettuce, cold-grown. 500 bunches of sorrel, cold-grown; average weight per bunch: 1 kilog. 500 gr. 500 bunches of parsley, cold-grown; average weight per bunch: 1 kilog. 10,500 feet of green romaine, cold-grown. 1,000 bunches of cold-grown caroites; average weight: 2 kilograms. 1,500 plants of cold-grown cauliflower. 8,000 plants of Georges and red lettuce, grown in costières. 6,000 vines of red lettuce, bedded. 3,500 feet of blond romaine in squares. 2,500 bunches of radishes, 3 handfuls each (the handful is the commercial radish bunch). 6,000 feet of cabbage-apples. 1,200 bunches of onions: average weight: 2 kilos. 1,200 bunches of seedling leeks; average weight: 2 kilos. 800 bunches of spring onions; average weight: 1 kilo. 1,200 bunches of spring onions; average weight: 1 kilo. 300 bundles of field sorrel; average weight: 1 kilo. 500. 500 callets de régence, harvested in the melon patch; average weight: 1 kilog. 1,200 bunches of carrots; average weight: 2 kilog. 200 bunches of chervil; average weight: 1 kilog. 200 bunches of civet; average weight: 400 gr. All the crops detailed above are harvested before July. The summer season begins at this time, and includes: 3,000 melon plants, on trenches with frames and cloches, and on beds with compost. 4,500 tomato plants, producing 5,000 callets of fruit for sale. 5,000 dozens of beautiful fruit. 30,000 plants of chicory, romaine and lettuce, obtained from the counter-planting of white celery. 10,000 plants of the same species, obtained from the counter-planting of escarole. 12,000 plants of escarole, also obtained in cauliflower squares by counter-planting. 120 small mannes of long pepper. 3,000 feet of autumn cauliflowers. 1,000 bunches of carrots, harvested in October. 1,000 bunches of transplanted leeks. This harvest ends in November, when the winter season begins, with crops including: 6,000 feet of celeriac, planted in June. 35,000 plants of white celery, planted in July. 600 callets of lamb's lettuce. 100 bundles of chervil. This sale ends in February, when the harvest and sale of early spring vegetables begins. It is obvious to everyone that the harvest we have just detailed can be more or less abundant, more or less good in quality, more or less productive, according to the needs and tastes of the day: the great variation in selling prices imposes absolute silence on this item. We should also mention the sale of 200 cubic meters of potting soil for disposal. We have seen from the above that the establishment has already cost: In operating equipment, round figures...................... 26,000 fr. To this must be added two sheds for the frames, and the vegetable greenhouse.................. 2,400 TOTAL....... 28,400 fr. These constructions are, in general, the property of the tenant. As for the annual costs, we know them personally; they add up... Horse feed costs....................... Shoeing and harness maintenance, veterinary care, cartwrighting. Rent, taxes, insurance and unforeseen expenses............. Staff food, maintenance and laundry... Frame repairs, bell replacement...... Coal for the machine.................. Pump for maintenance and repairs.................. Seeds for sowing, straw for binding........................ Callets for tomatoes and mâ2,170 fr. 1,200 200 100 2,500 6,600 500 200 109 160 ches....................... 600 Manure. Set........ 2,500 Total annual expenses... 16,830 The dwelling comprises one floor over ground floor and cellar, with living room, dining room, bedroom, kitchen and servants' quarters. Market gardeners rarely have a living room; everything else is necessary. Some market gardeners specialize in one crop, because they have a real superiority in it, and because the product compensates them for the care they give to the plant they have specially adopted, and even provides them with a considerable profit. It's up to everyone involved in horticulture to find their own path. In any case, the trade feeds its master, and that's the main thing. When the land is walled, the walls can be planted with vines, peach trees or apricot trees, which can be used to generate income or for staff consumption. Work distribution. The head of the establishment is absent as rarely as possible: his presence is necessary to direct and supervise the work, which he almost always shares. Getting up for him and for the dorestics specially attached to the cultivation usually takes place in summer at 5 a.m.; however, some jobs require getting up between 3.30 and 4 a.m., or even spending the whole night. When rising takes place in these exceptional circumstances, or when the night has had to be devoted to work, the necessary rest takes place during the heat of the following day. The housewife gets up between one and two o'clock, depending on the distance she has to cover to get to Les Halles. The three of them set off in the carriage loaded with products to be sold. The servant's role is to drive the car, unload the produce and return after loading manure in the stables, where it is bought by the year or month. The housewife makes her sale to the fruit-growers without moving from her place, while the daughter carries the goods sold in a hood to the carriages parked some distance away, which the fruit-growers have brought with them; this is how the delivery takes place. It's easy to see why such hard work calls for a first meal; the necessary bread is brought from home, and the rest of the food can be found around Les Halles. There's something for every taste. Without going into further detail, we can say, without fear of contradiction, that market gardeners feed their staff well, a necessary condition for the hard work and exceptional fatigue of their profession. For workers who stay at home, the first meal is at six o'clock in the morning, and generally consists of bread at discretion and 20 centilitres of wine. The second meal takes place at nine o'clock, when the entire staff is assembled. Immediately afterwards, the women and servants in charge of the Halle take a two-hour rest and then get down to work. The third meal takes place at two o'clock. This is immediately followed by cultivation work by all the men, and harvesting work by all the women. At seven o'clock, the next day's sale has to be prepared, so all the staff are busy loading, which is immediately followed by the fourth meal or supper, which immediately precedes bedtime. It's hardly before nine o'clock that the market gardener and his staff can take their well-earned rest. It's clear from the above that, in this profession, the woman's activity and intelligence is the first condition for success. In charge of sales, she must not only apply herself to getting a good product from what she brings, but also guess what the needs are and indicate to her husband the species of plant that is lacking on the market and whose cultivation would be advantageous. She must adorn her merchandise, that is, give it that coquettish look without which the most beautiful products go unnoticed, be affable with the customer and willingly lend herself to any services he may require to help with transport. On the way back, she thinks about the day's provisions for meals and other family needs. No sooner has she taken a few moments' rest than she has to supervise the kitchen, take care of the dishes and laundry, then preside over the making of the boots, mantles and callets for the merchandise, examining them one by one to see if her prescriptions have been well understood, the washing well done, the measure properly filled. All this may seem very difficult to accomplish, but it's all done every day, and households where the woman's activity and intelligence are lacking are the exception. In the establishment we have just reviewed, the new watering method is being put into practice. As a point of comparison, we'd like to give you the cost of the barrels if we'd stuck to the old system. It would still have required 550 meters of pipe. 1 large barrel................ 15 1 large stopcock.......... 30 70 barrels, supply and installation... 840 70 taps................... 700 70 meters of lead pipe... 210 4 pairs of watering cans............ 120 Total costs..... 2,465 fr. The sprinkler system for the same plot costs 2,540 fr. The difference is therefore the tiny sum of 75 francs, which is well repaid by 70 meters of land returned to cultivation, not to mention the savings in labor. Anyone intending to become a market gardener must complete an apprenticeship of at least four or five years; he must remain single until he feels ready to establish himself, and work for others during this time; his establishment as an owner dates from his marriage. It's not a matter of taking a wife from an indeterminate class of society; the first condition of success is that the woman he takes should be familiar with work and sales: so it's from among his daily workmates that the aspiring market gardener chooses his fiancée. This habit, which has perpetuated the customs of the old guilds among our class of workers, is one of the first necessities of the trade. The difference in the work of each of the spouses is so clear-cut that they cannot work without the other, and the death of one of the spouses almost always leads fatally to the disastrous sale of the business when this cruel separation comes too soon. To the husband: production; to the wife: selling the products and forecasting needs, in conditions that early experience must have prepared her for. Exposed from two o'clock in the morning to all the inclemencies of the seasons, forced to be constantly on guard against the tricks of the not always very scrupulous buyers at the Halles, obliged to think about and provide for the needs of the household before enjoying a few hours of well-earned rest, the market gardener's wife has no other satisfaction than to see her savings accumulate little by little and protect her family from need. She is the strong woman of Scripture: she reigns in the interior of her home, surrounded by the love of her family and the respect of those she occupies. What a hard life, even compared to that of simple workers in other professions! But what a difference in results! The market gardeners were right to preserve the esprit de corps that ensures daily life and prosperity for all their members. However, market gardeners' wives and daughters also have their days of coquetry and festivity. Saint-Fiacre's Day, always religiously celebrated, and wedding feasts provide opportunities to adorn themselves in pompous finery. It's then, after a hearty meal seasoned with good appetite and open merriment, that the ties that will later become the solid knots of a matched marige begin. It should be added that, as a result of this corporate structure, with all market gardeners forming a single family, marriages are made with full knowledge of the facts, and none of the spouses can be fooled by the illusions so common in unions contracted among other members of society. Once the marriage has been decided, it's time to find a suitable plot of land. If the couple is wealthy, they will do well to buy the land and equipment of a market gardener who is going out of business. It's true that this will put them into debt, but they'll only have one creditor to wait on. What's more, the equipment and manure will enable him to produce immediately, and therefore make money. If, in such a case, you wanted to buy new land, you'd have to buy the necessary equipment and manure at short notice, and you'd have to make large payments before you had a single product to sell: you'd be courting ruin. When it comes to buying land, you need to consult your means and balance them in such a way that the commitments you make do not exceed three quarters of the income less anticipated needs, the other quarter being intended as a reserve against unforeseen circumstances. New plots of land are only suitable for market gardeners with their own equipment and a sufficient quantity of manure and potting soil to immediately bring their gardens into production. To put things in perspective, we can say that funds below 5,000 francs and above 30,000 francs are rare. FERTILIZERS AND LAND WORKING The only fertilizer used for market gardening is manure from stables, or failing that, from cowsheds. Anything else would only imperfectly fulfil the purpose: the first condition, in fact, is to develop heat, the second to nourish the plants. Artificial fertilizers overloaded with nitrogen cannot fulfill the first condition, as the heat produced by their decomposition is insignificant due to their small volume. Market gardeners obtain manure from stables by means of subscriptions with transport contractors, omnibus and small carriage admistrations, cavalry regiment barracks, etc. The subscription is for a fee. Subscriptions are concluded for a fixed sum of so much per month and per horse, depending on the market price on the day of the contract and the quantity of manure. Police regulations prohibit horse owners from maintaining manure heaps in their yards beyond the quantity supplied daily by the stables, when this manure is not at a regulated distance from neighboring properties. Stable manure is supplied by feeders. Stable manure is colder than stable manure and decomposes more slowly, making it particularly suitable for siliceous soils, whereas stable manure is an indispensable ingredient for clay soils. The two manures should therefore be mixed in proportion to the amount of clay and silica in the soil. If you have enough stable manure, you can do without it, and it's even preferable to do without. As the soil is expensive and must produce as much as possible, the market gardener's manure bin, although containing a considerable quantity of this fertilizer, must take up as little space as possible, so it gains in height what it is denied in surface area. It is shaped like a quadrilateral, placed on a flat surface that is as impermeable as possible. In general, the length is no more than twice the width, and the height no more than two-thirds the length. Manure is piled up with a fork and trodden down with the foot as it is unloaded. A sort of chimney is built into the center of the heap, into which other horizontal ducts lead on all sides, so that fresh air penetrates the heap and neutralizes the excess heat produced by fermentation, which can go so far as to ignite the mass. All too often, this precaution is neglected, and fire breaks out in these fertilizers, which can only be extinguished by spreading out the heap; for water, which cannot penetrate the incandescent center, is little help against this kind of fire. In such cases, a season's harvest is lost, both through the loss of the manure itself and through the spreading. As market gardening establishments are located in the middle of homes or similar establishments, manure is spread at the expense of the premises where the fire occurs, and on land in full cultivation. By delaying decomposition, he preserves gas and heat for his crops, which would otherwise be lost to the atmosphere, to his detriment and that of his neighbors. This fire hazard means that manure piles should be kept away from dwellings. We place it at least 10 meters from the premises inhabited by the market gardener, so as to monitor it more easily and to quickly multiply the air holes if we feel the need. It should also be placed close to the well, so that help can be provided more effectively in the event of a disaster. As humidity encourages decomposition, and abundant water carries away soluble parts, it should be protected as much as possible from rainwater. To this end, we make it as wide as possible from base to top, slope the edges and line the top with the longest straw least penetrated by urine and excrement. The manure heap thus constructed contains fresh manure at the top, which will produce a lot of heat as soon as it is buried and wet, but will not become fertilizer until some time after burial, at least eight days. The second, intermediate part between the top and the bottom, contains half-hot, half-fertilized manure, which is ideal for planting delicate plants requiring both abundant warmth and nourishment. The other third is made up of manure, suitable for robust plants that require nothing more than nourishment. year on a small surface such as that of a market gardener's garden leaves behind a thick layer of humus known as compost. Potting soil is composed almost entirely of carbon, with a few particles of silica and clay; repeated watering has removed the most soluble parts. Used on its own, it is unsuitable for crops intended to be grown quickly, but mixed with soil it is an excellent fertilizer for cold crops. It is also a sales product for other crops. Manufacturers of artificial fertilizers, notably poudrette, buy potting soil, which acts both as an absorbent and as a disinfectant for faecal matter, thanks to the carbon it contains, and which finds new soluble combinations in this addition, once again becoming a powerful fertilizer for field crops. Flower growers also use it to make the compost known as "terre de bruyère"; to increase its fertility, they add colombine or poulaite. Finally, in ornamental gardens, the soil in baskets and flowerbeds is covered with it, to keep the soil fresh and to bring out the greenness of the foliage and the brightness of the flowers against the velvety black background. The market gardener retains only as much compost as he needs to cover his fertilizers and form a layer of topsoil in which his produce can take root. When the frames are moved, after the crops have been harvested in layers, we find, in addition to the compost, patches of straw that have not been completely destroyed and still contain soluble parts. This is known as "fond de couches" or mulch, and is a fertilizer that market gardeners often use and keep. This light, cold manure is used as a cover for spring seedlings; it keeps the young plant moist and protects it from the scorching spring sun and the night frosts of the same period. A few trials have been carried out in order to avoid the immense quantity of manure required for early crops. Layers without manure were created, heated in the same way as greenhouse tarpaulins, using a thermosiphon, i.e. a hot-water circulation system. The soil in the tarpaulin was then fertilized with a powerful fertilizer such as guano, colombine or poulaite. These trials produced results, but they were costly in terms of fuel and, above all, manpower; a worker was needed to accomplish the task that manure does on its own and at no cost. What's more, neither potting soil nor mulch could be produced for cold cultivation. These disadvantages led to the abandonment of these trials. The first condition for the existence of a market garden is a good supply of manure. The second is the intelligent use of this resource, which will be the subject of the following chapters. Preparatory work and ploughing for cold and hot cultivation. In discussing how to lay out a market garden, we said that it was divided into plots 21 meters long and 2.34 meters wide. Now let's suppose we have a new plot of land, and we'll prepare it for its intended use as follows. The land will be chosen in such a way as to have a well that is not too deep and that provides water at all times; if this condition cannot be easily met, a tube will be drilled and sunk deep enough to reach a good underground water table. The land will be levelled, if it is not already, and first ploughed to the depth of a spade-head. During this operation, all roots and weeds will be removed, and only then will the garden be laid out, starting with the hillside and counter-hillside as mentioned above. All the paths will be cordoned off, and the soil will be kept higher than that of the planks, so that the water from the moorings runs off well into the planks and not into the paths, where it would only make mud without profit. Once the first ploughing has been completed and the beds and frame squares have been laid out, the first cold harvest can be obtained in new soil, taking care to protect the surface of the soil with a good cover of half-consumed manure in spring, and compost at any other time. To this end, the necessary quantity of manure (5 to 10 centimetres thick) is brought to the smoking bed with a hood. The plank is spaded at one end and the earth is thrown away at the other. A first trench is thus opened, and a layer of manure is buried in it, to be covered by the earth removed with the spade for the second trench. The second trench is then filled with manure and covered by the soil from the third, and so on until the end. This ploughing method is only used for cold cultivation; the manure used comes from the bottom of the layers mixed with soil from the same source. After ploughing, the clods are broken up with a rake and the soil is levelled, taking care to remove any stones, roots or weeds found. This precaution is essential, especially in the case of mercurial and common spurge, commonly known as "foirolle" and "réveil-matin", plants which invade new land in surprising numbers. After this, the land is ready to receive the seedlings or plantations it is to receive. The choice of manure is not a matter of indifference, because if you put new manure in cold crops, you will have too much heat and the plant will grow too fast, then wither or even perish immediately without having vegetated; moreover, this method will make the weeds grow back whose seeds have not yet been destroyed by the fermentation of the manure. When potting soil is lacking, we make a floor from a light layer of manure, which we water abundantly to help it decompose rapidly. It is then used as mulch and cover. As soon as a bed is empty, it is ploughed again and fertilized in the same way. It's said that it takes three years to make a good marsh; this is true, but you shouldn't imagine that the land remains unproductive during this time; it simply means that after three years, there will be too much fertilizer and the market gardener will have potting soil to sell. If fertilizer is lacking, it will take twice as long. To make a hot layer, the soil is first ploughed with a spade, then levelled. On a bed 20 metres long and 1m 66 wide, a 40-centimetre-thick bed of old, dry horse manure is laid, well handled and stirred with a fork. This layer of manure is tamped down to bind and even it out, then covered 2. with a 25-centimeter-thick bed of potting soil. Continue the same work as many times as you want to make layers, but if you make only one layer, it must be at least two meters wide, including paths and shoulders. The paths are taken at the expense of the manure layer, and the shoulders are the manure accumulated at each end of the layer. To cultivate melons, we make what we call melon trenches; the bed is broken up with a spade and all the earth removed with a hood: we thus have a trench 20 meters long by 1m 66 wide, and Om 40 deep. This trench is filled with old, dry horse manure, or, if there's a shortage, with fresh manure mixed with half mulch and half potting soil: the latter method has the disadvantage of heating unevenly and, in places, giving off blasts of fire that destroy the plants by burning the roots. The whole, well watered and trampled, is covered with 20 centimetres of soil. Before laying the soil, a series of wooden boxes lined up end to end were placed on the trench, to be covered by fifteen glass frames. These boxes are fitted with feet at each of the four corners, and with crosspieces to maintain their spacing; their cross-section across the width is a right-angled trapezoid, with the large base facing north and the small base facing south. Each trench is covered by five trunks, each closed by three frames. These are oak frames, divided into three parts by small iron bars, to which the glazing is applied with pins and putty. Each sash is fitted with two handles to lift it; it is opened to the north and south by means of a rack generally cut from a flat piece of wood such as a piece of planking; the edge of the sash bears on one of the teeth of the rack, which rests on the edge of the box. This is called giving air. The temperature of a layer can be judged by plunging your hand into the ground. It can be determined using a thermometer. The space between two rows of frames is filled with manure, known as a pathway. New manure is used for hot cultivation, and is called "rechauds". Ados are boards sloping towards the south, on which spring and autumn seedlings are sown under cloches at a time when it is prudent to be wary of the north wind. Each ados is designed to receive three rows of cloches placed in staggered rows, after a provisional sowing of radishes or other plants. To make an ados, a chalk line is stretched in the direction in which it should slope, i.e. from sunrise to sunset, over a length of 20 meters. Beginning at one end of the chord, spade the soil to the distance of a spade shoe, and throw it back onto the bare part. This creates the beginning of an embankment. Once the first row has been completed, the second is started by throwing the soil back onto the first slope. We continue in this way until we have a sloping board 1.83 m wide and with a slope height of Om 33. As we have said, the slopes are designed to receive cloches, glass vases Om 33 in diameter and the same height, which owe their name to their particular shape. Cloches are used for seedlings and transplants in nurseries. The ados are laid out in such a way that they receive sun all day long on the shortest days, while their steep slope protects them from cold north winds and exposes them to hot south winds. Frames and ados should be fitted with a sufficient number of doormats to cover the glass during frosty periods at night. These permanently installed mats are rolled to the top of the slopes, so that they can be quickly unrolled as shade, and consequently frost, gains the board they are intended to cover. When a recent layer ceases to give heat, it's because it's too dry; a slight dampening soon brings the temperature back to the desired point by reviving the fermentation of the manure. Experience is the best guide in such cases. Harrowing in market gardens is almost always carried out with a three-pronged fork that is very close together; some practitioners use an instrument called a harrow, which we feel works less perfectly. Sowing is done either broadcast or in rows for all plants that must remain in place. To broadcast seed, the gardener holds a light handful of seed in his hand and lets it pass between his fingers, swinging his arm back and forth. A bed is usually sown with two handles, dividing the width in two. When the seed is very fine, there is a risk of sowing too thickly. This is avoided by mixing the seed with very dry compost. After sowing, the seed is harrowed with a fork, then the bed is tamped, an operation also known as ploughing. The edges of the bed are then covered with soil using a rake, and covered with a light layer of fine compost. If the weather is dry, the operation is finished off with a basining. lument in the same way. Row sowing is used for supplies. The line is drawn with a hoe or foot along a string. The seed is carefully scattered along the line; the soil is brought back to the furrow with both feet, then raked in; the potting soil is spread and basined as in the previous case. Nursery sowing is used for plants that cannot be sown in situ, such as lettuces and romaine. Under a frame, it's a simple broadcast sowing; under cloches, a pinch of seeds is sown; on ados, in the circumference traced by a bell that has first been placed there. As soon as the young plant has developed two leaves, pull out the seedling and prick it out with your finger, under a frame, five centimetres apart and staggered. Twenty to forty young plants are placed in the same way under a bell jar. As the young plants grow and get in each other's way, the distance between them is doubled. At the end of the transplanting process, no more than five and no less than three plants should remain under each bell, and no more than thirty and no less than twenty plants under each frame. Very young seedlings are transplanted with the finger, large seedlings with the planter; in all cases, the soil must be firmly pressed against the roots with the hand or planter. In market gardens, weeding is done by hand to avoid injuring plants close to the weeds to be removed. Hoeing is carried out as often as the soil hardens. Experience shows when and how it should be done. We call edging the operation that consists in raising a bank around the bells to support the soil with which they are loaded. The kerbstone is a plank applied along these embankments, which is tamped down to bind and solidify the soil. The muted layers are laid in trenches half as wide as the ordinary layers, they receive the same thickness of manure and are covered with a bed of earth. In very cold weather, hot cultivation layers need to be reheated. Ordinary paths and bunds are replaced with new manure, which is forked in every week, and new manure is added as soon as it becomes apparent that the heat is dropping. It's at the time of ploughing and bed reshuffling that you need to destroy the larvae of white grubs, moths, the larvae of earwigs known as fiddleheads, which destroy a good number of lettuce plants in spring, and the earwig, which destroys cauliflowers. Many ways have been suggested to destroy these enemies of plantations, the best of which is to pick them up by hand and kill them. Slugs and snails are another scourge. They are hunted down every day, as are caterpillars. Ants rarely establish themselves in marshes, and are rarely seen except in new fields. CULTIVATION TOOLS AND INSTRUMENTS MEANS OF MAINTAINING AND PRESERVING THEM Everyone is familiar with the spade; there are several shapes; the one we prefer is 36 centimetres high, 22 centimetres wide at the top, and 18 centimetres at the tip: it is attached to the handle by a socket, and the blade is very lightly girdled. The hoe, iron rake, rake, chalk line and planter deserve no special mention. The loader is a kind of horizontal T mounted on three 80-centimeter-high legs. The longest leg of the T carries two horn-shaped pieces of wood spaced apart. The loader is used to support hoods loaded with manure or vegetables. Carriages known as jardinières belong to the tapissière family; they are suspended on two wheels. For transporting manure, a cart is used which is suspended when it is used for two purposes, i.e. when it has to carry manure back from the hall. If it's used only for manure, no springs are used. Mechanically-made doormats are now more regular, more convenient and just as inexpensive as the doormats that used to be made by hand; however, for people who are far from manufacturing centers, it's a good idea to know how to make doormats yourself. For this purpose, we use a wooden frame 2 meters long and 1.33 meters wide. Three pegs are fixed to each end bar. With the frame thus laid out, we stretch a string from bottom to top over each corresponding peg; the top string being stopped on the peg by a slipknot, we leave it an excess length equal to twice the length of the frame. Once this is done, we spread a very even layer of rye straw over the frame, then a second layer of the same straw, with the ears pointing in the opposite direction to those of the first; we take the string holding the middle peg, and wind it onto a piece of wood which serves as a shuttle. Separate a pinch of straw by passing the shuttle from right to left over the straw and under the string, then pass it back into the loop formed earlier, and tighten the knot by flattening it between thumb and forefinger, and so on to the end where the string is stopped by a knot. The same procedure is followed for the two rows at the ends, and when the doormat is finished, the ears of corn are cut on each side to even out the edges. Doormats are made on rainy days and winter evenings, when it's impossible to attend to outside work. There are two types of sash boxes: they can either be made in one piece, or they can be dismantled. The former are made as follows: Take four pieces of 8-centimeter squared oak, cut two to 35-centimeter lengths, and the other two to 30-centimeter lengths. On the first two pieces, nail the two ends of a 30-centimeter-wide by 3.90-meter-long plank, leaving 5 centimeters of foot space for the oak pieces. On the other two pieces, we nail a plank 25 centimetres wide and 'm 90 long, leaving a further 5 centimetres free. The first board and its two feet form the back of the trunk, while the second board and its feet form the front. To close the trunk, we join them together by nailing a plank to each end, so that with all four feet in the same place, the upper part should slope 5 centimetres over a length of 1m 40. Since the quadrilateral thus formed is to accommodate three frames and a heavy load of soil, it's clear that these boards would warp if they weren't prevented by two bars, dividing the trunk into three equal parts, each of which is intended to be covered by a frame. These bars are cut into dovetails at each end, which fit into notches made in the boards for this purpose. When not in use, these chests are kept in the garden in the shade, and along a wall as far as possible; they are stacked dozens or dozens on top of each other, and last for five or six years. The short lifespan of fixed boxes, and the inconvenience of having to leave them unprotected after they have been in use, has led to the adoption of boxes that can be dismantled, and which, although initially requiring a larger outlay of capital, actually cost less, since they take up less space when not in use, and can be sheltered from the inclemency of the seasons. Market gardeners use two types: gambrel boxes and crutch boxes. The gambille consists of a 1.40-meter oak crossbeam nailed to two unequal legs, one 30 to 35 centimeters long, the other 20 to 25. Each of the legs carries an iron staple or hook on the outside, and the boards of the trunk engage in this staple by means of two notches. To cover a layer of fifteen frames, you need four gambilles and two heads for each end. These heads each consist of a plank, cut to match the slope, and mounted on two legs, each bearing a hook. The crutch is the essential part of the sliding chest. It's a 1.40 m long oak crosspiece nailed at each end to two solid legs of unequal length. These legs are notched, the front leg 11 centimeters deep, the rear 16 centimeters. These notches are the same thickness as the boards they are designed to accommodate. Two strong legs join each crutch to its two feet. Two plank ends are joined in the notch, and the planks are notched so that they fit snugly together. Ten boards are needed for each layer of fifteen frames. The heads are nailed to two notched feet like those of the crutch. The crutch trunk is superior to the gambrel trunk because of its simplicity and ease of repair; it is also less expensive and easier to assemble and disassemble, as the parts have less adherence to each other. The main disadvantage of the gambrel chest is that the wood has swollen with humidity, and the iron parts hold them so tightly together that they cannot be dismantled without damaging some of them; crutch chests, being made entirely of the same material, do not have this disadvantage. The bar is one of the most important parts of the chest, and it is generally at the point where the bars are assembled that the rotting of the chest boards begins. The tail bar, which requires a deep notch, must therefore be rejected; it has been replaced by the staple bar, fitted at each end with a right-angled iron plate attached Chassis Barre à queue Tête de Coffre à Gambilles Barre à Agrafe FI Tête de Coffre à Béquilles 4 Barre à Crochet R4II Mear A Lagnon. Lopont " D D D G G D D D D D D D D D . D D D D D D G D 1 the bar with two screws. The board is only notched a few millimeters thick, i.e. the thickness of the small iron plate. The use of the staple bar is obviously an improvement, but it does have its drawbacks: the staple is too weak to bear a heavy load, and after a few years, the screws play into the wood and the plate comes loose without the bar being able to be used again. The author of this book has invented a system of hooked bars, which he has been using with advantage for some time now. It consists of counter-soldering an iron plate in such a way that the hook screws are applied from underneath the bar. The plate is applied to the standing wood by a first right-angled bend, a second brings it back in the direction of the bar, and finally a third completes the hook. This system enables the bar to withstand heavy loads without inconvenience. Each hook costs just 12 to 15 centimes. You can do it yourself, but you need to take precautions, as strip iron is very brittle. You can be sure of success by reddening the iron and bending it onto a specially-made core. Market gardening frames all have the same shape and dimensions: they are rectangles measuring 1.40 m long by 30 m wide. They are glazed with twelve equal panes and four half-panes of half-double glass at the bottom, and have a handle on each opposite bar along the length. The oldest model is made entirely of oak and costs 12 or 1,300 francs with its box. It provides good service, and well-maintained paintwork lasts from fifteen to twenty years. Preference is given to an oak-framed sash, the small timbers of which are replaced by T-iron bars. One hundred frames of this model cost 1,400 to 1,500 francs with their boxes. They have the advantage over their predecessors of being less shady in the trunks and of lasting a few years longer. A third type of frame has three oak bars and the fourth, lowest bar is made of double-T iron. One hundred frames of this model are worth 1,600 to 1,700 francs. It is superior to the previous models in that the bottom bar, which is most exposed to humidity, rots very quickly in wooden-framed sashes. It therefore requires fewer repairs and lasts longer. Frames made entirely of iron are not used for market gardening: their high price, their weight, which makes them difficult to maneuver, and the conductivity of iron, which causes too much heat in the sun and heat loss in the dark, all combine to make market gardeners reject this type of frame. Before the tiles are laid, the frames are given two coats of ceruse white, crushed with linseed oil and petrol, and the brilliance of the white is extinguished with a little lampblack. The glazing putty is applied in the same shade with linseed oil and Spanish white mixed with black. A third coat is applied to the top after this putty has been applied. The frames should be returned to the shed as soon as the glass is no longer needed, i.e. as soon as the plants grown under them no longer fear the cold of the night. Beforehand, we take care to accustom them to the outside temperature by giving them plenty of air. Under the sheds, stacks of fifty frames are formed, sometimes more, depending on the height of the shelter and the space available, but the lower the stacks, the more manageable they are. The chests are left on the ground for a while, then stacked in the open air in the shade. Ten to twelve chests are stacked one on top of the other; if any can be dismantled, they are stored in the shed whenever possible, otherwise they are piled outside with their accessories, and secured with a layer of bedding. When the rains give us time to spare, we iron the frames one by one, replacing any missing glass or putty, as the market gardener needs to know how to use a diamond like a glazier; a coat of oil paint on top and underneath completes the maintenance work. It is customary, when removing bells before tightening them, to put one inside the other. This is a bad habit, because the inside of the bells is more or less damp, and this is enough to make the two touching glass surfaces stick together, and cause breakage when you try to free them. It's a good idea to set the bells upright one on top of the other; a small wooden cube is placed on top of the first, which is removed, and the edges of the second, which is placed on top, cannot touch those of the first; a new cube is placed on top of the second, so it can receive a third bell. All must be placed on the wooden cube in such a way that they can rotate freely without touching the glass of the bell below. Before clamping them permanently, they are washed one by one, and placed, as we have just described, in a shed or even in the open air, they are covered with litter and old mats to prevent shocks which could break them. A broken bell is discarded only when it cannot be repaired. 4. Bells that have been broken into several large pieces are repaired when these juxtaposed pieces only form cracks that need to be reattached in a solid manner. For this purpose, pieces of glass are cut into small squares, called pins, and white ceruse ground in oil is added to a little minium to speed up drying; one side of each pin is coated with this preparation and applied externally across the crack; the whole length of the crack is thus lined with pins from distance to distance. The preparation is left to dry thoroughly before the bell is stirred, making it as strong and usable as a new one. Doormats are returned to the shed as soon as the season allows their use to be discontinued. Care should be taken to expose them first to dry air, and to ensure that they are no longer damp. Sprinkle them with fine ash to absorb any remaining water and protect them from insect attack; then roll or stack them until they are needed again. Temperature is an essential thing to know; our senses can deceive us, but the thermometer never does. We have the bad habit of hanging the thermometer along a wall and using the primitive thermometer fixed on a wooden board. The only admissible instrument that gives accurate readings is the glass thermometer, which can be suspended from its top so that the air envelops it completely. Observations should be made in the middle of the garden, in the prevailing wind, one in the shade, the other in the sun. This thermometer is suspended by a cord from the end of a small gallows. There's no need to keep it stationary, and it can be used for other useful indications. To judge the temperature of a layer, the grower usually uses his hand, and this is sufficient in practice. However, as it may sometimes be useful to determine a constant temperature for certain crops, it would be a good idea, in experiments intended for publication, to use the very thermometer whose use we recommend. Almost all market gardeners have at home, and with good reason, the Vidi dial barometer, which indicates atmospheric pressure and depression with sufficient accuracy for their needs. However, the bad habit of striking the dial to set the needle in motion should be avoided, as this causes mercury to escape from the bowl, thereby destroying the correspondence between the needle's position and the barometric height. A more convenient barometer is the Bourdon. It takes up less space, is more sensitive and, as it contains no mercury, can be set in any position without being disturbed. An equally useful instrument is the hygrometer, which indicates the degree of dryness and humidity in the atmosphere. It's a simple fixed point, wound around a movable pulley and terminated by a small weight. A needle is attached to the pulley shaft. Air saturated with water vapour causes the rope to stretch, absorbing its moisture; dry air, on the other hand, causes it to shrink, evaporating the water vapour it contained. The needle moves in one direction or the other as the rope lengthens or shrinks. The two extreme points indicate maximum and minimum air humidity. Commercially, the support for this device is shaped like a capuchin, with a movable cap serving as a needle. The cap covers the head when the string lengthens, meaning rain or thaw; it uncovers the head when the string narrows, meaning drought, heat or frost. CULTIVATION Seventeen families of plants provide the vegetables strictly necessary to supply the market halls with this branch of industry. 1° Liliaceae provide garlic, asparagus, spring onions, civets, shallots, onions and leeks. 2° Polygonaceae provide sorrel and rhubarb. 3° Chenopodaceae provide spinach, pear, arrowroot and beet. 4° Solanaceae supply tomatoes, chillies, eggplants and potatoes. 5° Labiatae supply basil, savory and thyme. 6° Borraginaceae supply borage. 7° Companulaceae supply raiponium. 8° Compositae supply artichokes, cardoons, lettuces, chicories, escarole, romaine, dandelions, salsify and scorzonera. 9° Valerian plants provide lamb's lettuce and regency. 10° Umbellifers provide carrots, celery, parsnips, chervil and parsley. 11° Crucifers provide cabbages, cauliflowers, watercress, mustard, turnips, radishes and turnips. 12° Tropaeolae provide nasturtiums. 13° Portulaceae provide purslane. 14° Ficoides provide tetragonia. 15° Rosaceae supply strawberries and burnet. 16° Legumes include beans, lentils, peas and broad beans. 17° Cucurbits include melons, cucumbers, gherkins, squash and pumpkins. To these families can be added a grass, maize; a bromeliad, pineapple; a convolvulaceous, sweet potato; a dioscorea, Chinese yam; an oxalid, crenellated oxalis; and finally, a fungus of the agaric genus, the mushroom. With the exception of the pineapple and the mushroom, which form the object of special cultivation and an important trade, and of the corn, cultivated especially for vinegar makers, these last plants are hardly to be found except in the vegetable gardens of large houses or with market gardeners who have assured themselves in advance of their certain sale. For the special characteristics of families, we refer our readers to botanical treatises; it seems sufficient to us to say that plants of the same family generally have the same needs; and, given a plant of a given family, even if the cultivation of this plant is unknown, we can a priori succeed by giving it the cultivation of the plant whose botanical characteristics it most closely resembles. Once this is understood, we will follow the alphabetical order for the special cultivation of each plant, because it is the most convenient for research. Garlic. Garlic is native to Sicily. It is a bulbous plant of the Liliaceae family; in our climate, it multiplies by cloves planted 4 or 5 centimetres apart. Planting takes place in February or March in loose, moderately smoky soil. They can also be planted in rows, which facilitates the weeding and hoeing this plant needs to thrive. Harvesting takes place in July or August. The plants are left to dry on the ground, then tied into bunches and kept hanging in a dry place. Only as many bulbs as are needed for the following year's planting are kept; the rest are sold. Garlic is a seasoning plant that awakens the appetite when used in small quantities, but becomes indigestible and gives bad breath when abused. Market gardeners cultivate this plant only rarely and in small quantities. Pineapple. See Special crops below. Arroche. Arroche or belle-dame is a chenopod native to Tartary. It is sown from March to September, from month to month, in the midst of other plantations. Sow very lightly, and pull up the excess if sown too thickly. Wet the plant moderately, and only in dry weather. Pick the leaves, which can be eaten cooked like spinach or mixed with sorrel to sweeten it. There are two varieties: blond and red. The seeds are harvested before maturity from the most beautiful plants, which are set aside for this purpose. They are dried in the shade away from the wind, as they are very light. Seeds do not retain their germinative power beyond one year. Artichoke. Artichokes are native to southern Europe and are propagated by eyelets or suckers in March or April. As far as possible, roots are removed from the eyelets, and the leaf tips are cut off. Prepare well-ploughed and, above all, well-smoked beds, and mark out rays 80 cm apart. The artichokes are planted in these rays, 70 to 80 centimetres apart in a chessboard pattern. Each eyecup should be planted by hand, tightly packed and with the roots well elongated; once planted, each plant is given a little water, which makes it easier to take root. We can make them more precocious by planting them in small pots placed in a chessboard under a frame on a warm layer. When the roots have developed sufficiently to fill the pot, each plant is planted in place. Artichokes take up a lot of space and grow slowly, so make the most of the considerable space left between plants. Savoy cabbages or white onions can be planted; radishes or lettuce can be sown in the space; in any case, these sowings and counter-plants should not be long-lasting. An artichoke plantation should last three to four years. Every year, a young plantation is planted, the produce of which is harvested in autumn after that of the old plants. When frosts are imminent, the longest leaves are removed, the plants are spaded and surrounded with dry manure. On larger farms, the plant is spaded and covered with leaves; the leaves must then be covered with earth to prevent them being blown away. In March, we uncover the plants, remove the eyelets for young plantations and bury the manure used as a cover. Artichoke seed is good for five years; it is rarely harvested in our climate, as the eyelets are sufficient for reproduction. Seedbeds should be grown in the middle of the day, sheltered from the north winds. It is the unopened flower of the artichoke that is sold as edible; it is the spiny sepals of the calyx and the disk that form what are known as the artichoke leaves and heart. The rudimentary flowers form the hay. The few market gardeners who grow early artichokes obtain the first heads for sale in April, by covering old plants with frames and surrounding them with stoves. Ordinary harvesting takes place between May and June, and young plants in early autumn. Asparagus is a lily of central Europe; the variety we cultivate seems to have originated near Abbeville. Asparagus is grown in squares or banks laid out in such a way as to accommodate frames. The seed is sown in a nursery in March. The young plant is transplanted in place, 8 to 10 centimetres apart. The soil has been heavily smoked beforehand. For three years, radishes and spinach are grown in the asparagus plants in spring; lettuces, romaine, cauliflower and apple cabbage are planted in the paths; the asparagus plant benefits from watering, and grows flowers and seeds. It's a good idea to remove the seeds, which exhaust the plant. At the end of September or beginning of October, cut the stalks low to the ground and replenish the banks with soil. If you don't force the plant with manure, you can harvest in the spring of the third year. When you want to force asparagus, you 4 This transplanting takes place in April. place specially-built boxes on the banks in the third year, and cover them with a good bed of soil or compost. This work is done from the first days of November. The paths are dug to a depth of 5 centimetres, and the soil is thrown back on top of the compost to fill half the coffers. The pathway pit is then filled with mixed manure, which is well tamped down to the level of the frames. For the duration of the cultivation, this manure must be reworked every twelve or fifteen days; it is stirred with a fork and a small quantity of hot manure is added. Don't give the asparagus any air; at night, the frames should be covered with straw mats. A crop of asparagus is best cut after twenty to twenty-five days, after which it should be picked every two or three days. Once the crop has been harvested, the boxes used for reheating are only slowly and carefully removed, and everything is restored to its original state as soon as there is no longer any reason to fear a sudden change in temperature. Only half the asparagus plants are reheated each year, so as not to exhaust them too much. The crop we have just described is white asparagus. It can also be obtained by obtaining asparagus claws, which are placed on a warm bed at the same time, and from which the shoots are picked as they grow, but the plant is completely lost after harvesting. Growers around Paris sow asparagus in nurseries in February or March, then plant it in soil prepared as follows: they make trenches 1 meter wide, leaving a width of 1 meter between two trenches. These trenches are 20 centimetres deep. The soil is thrown back onto the in-between trenches. The bottom of the trench is filled with manure or gadoue (Paris mud). Three rows are planted per trench, spaced 30 to 35 centimetres apart. The whole is maintained by hoeing and weeding in summer and covering with manure in spring. The in-between areas are planted with oats, cabbage, beans, potatoes and beet for two years. In the third year, the soil is removed and spread over the plant to increase the length of the shoots. Harvesting takes place in April, May and June, taking care not to ruin the plantation, which can produce crops for ten years. It is these asparagus plants that provide the claws for the horticulturists who heat the green asparagus known as pea asparagus. There are currently two practical ways of achieving this, one using layered manure, the other using thermosiphon. These two processes are used with outstanding success by two Clichy horticulturists: Messrs Vassou and Cauconnier. We will therefore describe their processes. Mr. Vassou has created a special set of boxes and frames. The height of these boxes differs from those normally used. Towards the end of October and the beginning of November, he starts by 8. making layers 60 to 70 centimetres high of good mixed manure. Once the trunks are in place, the layer is covered with a few centimeters of soil and compost. Once fermentation is established and the hand immersed in the layer feels 18 to 20 degrees of heat, the claws are placed close together. It doesn't take long for vegetation to appear, so we cover the shoots with potting soil and put up the frames. If the weather is mild, air is let in; at night, straw mats are put down for fear of frost. If the temperature is too harsh, we put heaters in the paths. This type of cultivation produces until March. Mr. Cauconnier has installed powerful thermosiphons on his farm. This appliance consists of a hearth, an enclosed boiler, three-quarters full of water at 100 degrees when the appliance is in operation, i.e. when the furnace is lit. A siphon-shaped cast-iron pipe plunges into the water, exits the boiler in the direction marked out, and returns to the boiler after a circuit of varying length. When the boiler is closed, the steam produced by boiling compresses the surface of the water, which rises in the siphon-shaped tube. The water thus displaced travels through the entire circuit, cooling as it moves away from the furnace, and returns to the boiler, where it regains heat and begins the same journey again. The heat released by cooling the water replaces the manure in Mr. Cauconnier's eulture system. Instead of chests, he uses a masonry greenhouse covered with double glazing. The air trapped between the two frames forms a mattress, preferable to a doormat for keeping out the cold. At the bottom of the greenhouse run the thermosiphon pipes, above which a shallow wooden or sheet-metal tarpaulin filled with earth holds the asparagus shoots to be heated. The temperature is regulated by a thermometer. The work is the same as that described above. It begins in October and ends in March. Towards the end of October, the seed-bearing fruit is harvested from beautiful vines that have been staked. The fruit is crushed in water to separate the seed from the pulp, and the seed is dried in the shade. It can be used for up to four years. Eggplant. Eggplant is a member of the solanaceae family, native to the tropical regions of the Old and New Worlds. It is sown in January and February on a warm bed. Once the plant has emerged, it is transplanted to another layer with boxes and frames. This layer is made of mixed manure covered with a bed of compost, and accommodates 200 to 300 plants per frame. It is protected from the cold at night by a straw mat. In May, the same procedure is used to prepare a third layer for planting eggplants. This layer is covered with sashes or bells; if sashes are used, nine chessboard feet are planted under each sash; if bells are used, one foot is planted under each bell. When eggplants are planted in May, there's no need to worry about frost; they're planted on an old carrot bed or in a costiere, along a wall, so that they're well sheltered. Only five or six fruits should be kept on each plant; those on branches that are too weak should be removed, and buds that could sap the fruit should be cut off. Repeated watering and hoeing are necessary. There are two varieties of eggplant: round and long; this difference only applies to the fruit, which is a more or less intense purple. These fruits are edible after cooking, and are eaten stuffed. The seeds of the most beautiful fruits are preserved, dried in the shade and used for up to seven years. This is a labiatae native to India, which is sown in March or April on a drained bed, and transplanted in the nursery on another specially prepared bed. In May or June, the plants are planted on old beds or in the open ground. If possible, keep the root ball to facilitate recovery. This plant requires frequent wetting. The seed rarely reaches maturity in our climate; it is brought in from the Midi. It lasts for six years. This plant is used as a seasoning. Beet. This plant is a goosefoot native to southern Europe, which is sown in April and May in rows or broadcast, in well-smoked, deeply-ploughed soil; the seedling is thinned out as soon as the leaves touch, leaving a distance of about 25 cm between each plant. The plants are hoeed several times, and in October or November, the roots are pulled out after the leaves have been removed. These roots are kept in the vegetable greenhouse to protect them from frost. Beet is used as an edible plant, after cooking. The seeds, which last five years, are harvested from two-year-old plants. The healthiest roots are replanted in early spring. They soon provide a stem that rises, flowers and bears fruit in August or September. Planting for seed should take place in a south-facing position, and the plant should be frequently moistened to stimulate vegetative growth. Borage. A native plant in the Borage family, borage is sown in spring in the open ground. To have borage all year round, sow in September, prick out in rows in October, cover with a frame or straw mat and harvest in January or February. In January, sow in the ground and transplant onto an old bed. Borage is used as a medicinal plant. Its flower is used to decorate salads and is eaten with them. Borage seed is harvested a little before maturity and left to dry in the shade; it lasts for three years. Baselle or American spinach. This plant is native to tropical regions and belongs to the Basellaceae family. It is sown in March on a bed or in good exposure when frosts are no longer to be feared. The leaves are harvested in the same way as spinach, and have the same uses. The seed, which is brought in from the south because it does not ripen in the Paris climate, lasts three years. Nasturtium. Nasturtium is a tropalea native to Peru that is sown in April at the foot of a wall, or better still, on a trellis in good exposure. It can also be sown on its own, but must be raked. The flower is edible and is used to dress salads, giving them a pleasantly piquant flavor. Its green fruit can be preserved in vinegar. The seed is harvested in August and thoroughly dried before being stored. It lasts five years. Cardoon. A member of the Compositae family native to the island of Candia. It is sown in April on an old bed and the young plant is transplanted in May at a sufficient distance from the soil to later raise the cardoon clods. If you want to sow on the spot, place two or three seeds 80 centimetres apart in two rows per bed in holes filled with potting soil. When the seeds have emerged, leave only the most vigorous plant in each spot and remove the others. In the early stages, the potting soil is used for a counter-planting of romaine and chicory, as cardoons are very slow-growing. Towards the end of October, the cardoons have reached their peak vegetation, and it's time to blanch them. To this end, the leaves are lifted and tied with straw ties. As this plant has formidable thorns and the leaves are very thick, the leaves are lifted as follows: two people hold a stick in one hand and the end of a straw tie in the other. They lift the leaves with the stick and pass the straw tie underneath, successively going around the plant; when the tie is all the way around the collar, one of the people tightens the tie and ties it. The plant should be swaddled in this way, leaving only the leaf tips free. If the plant is to be blanched outside, we surround each foot with dry litter held in place by two ties. Leave in this state for three weeks. Otherwise, they are taken down to the cellar at the first threat of frost, and can be kept there throughout the winter. The seed-bearing plants are transplanted to a good exposure during the winter and secured with a good bedding. In spring, they are uncovered and watered frequently. Soon the flowers, which are almost identical to those of the artichoke, appear. The seed is harvested like that of the artichoke, and lasts for seven years. Carrot. The carrot is a native member of the Umbelliferae family. It is a market garden plant par excellence. The most widely grown variety is the chassis carrot, which is short and almost round. In early season, it is sown in beds in November. We're going to describe in detail how to set up the beds so that we don't have to come back to the subject later. The bed is set up in the best exposed spot in the garden, as close as possible to the house and manure storage area, to avoid wasting time and unnecessary effort. If the land is new, the soil is ploughed deeply, levelled, trampled and raked. If you don't have enough potting soil, mix it with soil by half at most. Mix as evenly as possible with a shovel. Pure potting soil is preferable, as plantations will suffer if soil is mixed, and if pure soil is used, as is too often the mistake in new establishments, four or five bad years will pass until good potting soil is available. It's a good idea to leave the soil 20 to 30 centimetres deep below the paths, to keep the layer warm. Five banks of potting soil for fifteen frames are formed across the square, making a length of 21 meters. Each bank is used to load three frames. Once the potting soil or mixture of soil and potting soil has been raised to the banks, any excess is removed. The manure is placed between the banks. If it's dry, we place it in advance to moisten it. It is handled to break it up - this is called a manure chain - and then the banks are covered with compost and manure.
