The first layer is isolated and 2 meters wide to serve as a gauge. For the others, we first establish a chain of old, dry manure, which we cover with a chain of new manure; we carefully mix these manures and compact them with the feet so that no gaps remain, and we cover with dry manure to prevent the seeds of the new manure from rising through the potting soil with which we will load the layer. The trunks should be placed square to the ground, with 20 to 25 centimetres of potting soil piled on top, spread out and pressed against the boards with the foot. The layers are then ready for sowing. Once the seedlings have been sown, they are evenly harrowed with a fork to prevent the seeds from forming a heap. The surface is then tapped with a flat board to tighten the seeds. Planting begins as soon as the layers have reached a suitable temperature, which is not long after the frames cover the trunks. A few days later, the paths are filled with manure, the beds are made, and the beds are reshaped whenever necessary. To speed up the work on the layers, we believe it's even preferable to the one we've just described, which is the one most generally used. After leaving 2 meters of width for the first gauge layer, we take a bank of potting soil lengthwise; we remove extra, then we make the banks for each layer by cutting the potting soil in a straight line with a string at the boundary of each layer; we shovel it up on each side and bring dry manure to each in-between. In this way, the soil and compost are protected from frost. The new manure is brought in over the top, and the work is carried out without interruption, the soil for loading the layer being within reach, whereas in the and you have to bring in the chains of dry manure and those of new manure separately. The result is greater speed and less fatigue. Layer manures are not wetted, except in February and March when they are too dry. Cloche layers are made in the same way as chassis layers. Each layer of fifteen frames should hold one hundred and twenty to one hundred and thirty bells. These layers should be edged with a board or a trunk board, so that two people can edge them more quickly. The soil must be well levelled before sowing, which is harrowed and tamped as we said for the frames. Planting takes place as soon as the bed warms up. The bells are placed in three rows, chalked and chessboarded. Then, as for chassis layers, the paths and shoulders are made with dry manure. Having said that, let's get back to the carrots. The carrot beds are 50 to 60 centimetres thick with mixed manure, and are fitted with their own boxes and frames. Thirty to thirty-six lettuces are planted in the seedbed to ensure a double harvest. Chassis are covered only during hard frosts. No air is given until the lettuce is ready to harvest, which takes place in January. Once the lettuce has been removed, air is given as needed and the frames are covered during frosts. Harvesting takes place in late March or early April. Carrots continue to be sown in layers every month until the first of March, and these layers are covered with frames or bins as required. A few spinach seeds can be sown at the same time as the carrot seed, and harvested after the lettuce. Radishes are often sown, but this is not always profitable, as radishes like air and lettuce fears it. In this case, it's better to do away with the lettuce and sow only radishes. In April, all the carrot beds are uncovered, and glass is needed to grow melons. Some time before uncovering the carrots, you need to get them used to the outside temperature by frequently giving them air. If the weather turns cold when the carrots are uncovered, they are covered overnight with mats, which are carried on strings held taut by stakes, so as not to crush the leaves. When sowing under cloches, plant three black lettuces and one green romaine per cloche. The lettuces can be removed and replaced with radishes. In this case, plant romaine between the cloches; those under the cloches will grow and be ready for planting.

are the first to be harvested: each bell is then placed on top of one of those outside. Carrots are still sown on open-air beds with radishes, and covered during frosts with mulch supported by small trellises. From January onwards, carrots are sown in the open ground under cold frames and cloches. If they were sown earlier, they would grow taller. Thirty plants of George or Gotte lettuces are planted per frame, otherwise they are replaced by twenty Romaines. If sowing was done under cloches, a good bed of compost should have been used, and three romaine or Gotte lettuces should be planted per cloche. In February, carrots are sown in pots with radishes, and lettuces or romaine are planted in the seedbed. Sowing continues from month to month until July 15, combining carrots with radishes or spinach, and planting romaine, lettuce and even celeriac in the seedbed. For beautiful, healthy carrots, we recommend planting only lettuces and romaine in the seedbed, and not combining the seed with any other. Carrots require good composting in spring and light mulching in summer. They need to be moistened in small quantities and often. The last ones sown for winter consumption can be kept in the ground, taking care to cut off the tops and cover the bed with a good bed of dry manure. If not, we pull them out, make a hole in the ground where we pile them up and cover them with dry manure. In autumn, we select the seed-bearing plants, which are the most beautiful and roundest, and place them in a nursery in a sheltered spot or under a frame. In March, they are planted outdoors, 35 centimetres apart. The seed is harvested in July or August. It lasts four years. Everyone knows the uses of carrots. The best varieties, after the short round one, are the early red from Holland and the half-long one. It is an indigenous umbellifer whose cultivated varieties are white celery, Turkish celery, purple celery from Tours, celery root and hollow or knife celery. The first three varieties are grown in exactly the same way. Hot-sowing of these varieties begins in late February and early March. Celery is transplanted in rows in April. It is good for harvesting in August and September. Second sowings take place a month later, with clear sowing and thinning of the plant instead of transplanting. Harvesting takes place in October and November. The third sowing takes place in the open ground from April 25 to May 6. The seedling is left in the nursery and thinned out if too thick. This last sowing will be sold during the winter. Celery is planted in lettuce, romaine or chicory beds that are half-planted. After planting, the plant is wetted to create a border. Celery requires a lot of water, but you mustn't give it more than it needs, as it will become too tender and contract the disease known as grise. The first celery to be sold can be blanched by covering it with old straw mats or bedding. For winter celery, make a trench 1.33 meters wide and 25 centimeters deep, discarding the soil to the right and left; plant the celery that has been removed in clumps in rows 15 to 20 centimeters apart, then wet abundantly to prevent the leaves from wilting. When they start to grow again, soil is poured between the rows until only the tips of the leaves are out. If you're not afraid of frost, it's a good idea to bury the celery only halfway and not to complete the operation until a little later, but you should bury it right away if the weather is doubtful. To raise the celery, once it has been simply placed in the trench where it is to be blanched, which happens when there is no fear of frost, two men, each equipped with a stick the width of the plank and two stakes, take a row of celery between their two sticks and straighten it. Once this is done, they secure the sticks with their two stakes, then spade earth between the separated rows. It's a good idea to cover the leaves of buried celery with a bed of dry manure when the frost is heavy. Celery is harvested from seed from the best plants of the previous year, transplanted along a wall in good exposure. It starts to grow in spring, and the seed is harvested in August; it lasts for seven years. Celery is eaten as a salad. Celery root differs from the preceding varieties in that it has a very fleshy root, which develops at the expense of the leaves, which are spindly and not very tall. Sow hot from March 1st on a good bed, transplant at the end of April on an old bed or in the ground. Combined with carrots, romaine or escarole, it needs to be planted light, with 30 to 40 cm between plants, and watered frequently. It's more harmful than useful to remove the leaves, as we do too often. A good mulch is an excellent precaution for celeriac. Two varieties are grown: white and red. The red variety produces larger roots, but is more prone to the disease known as grise. Celery root is used for the same purposes as the leaves of the above varieties, and is harvested from seed in the same way. Care must be taken to isolate seed carriers from other celery species, which are also harvested from seed, as hybridizations produce no good results. Hollow or knife celery is less sensitive to cold than the preceding varieties. It is sown hot in February, and on the ground in March. If you cover the seedbed with a frame, you'll be able to pick celery earlier. It is used as an ingredient in salads and stews. It is harvested from seed like other celery. Cerfeull. A native umbelliferous plant, chervil can be sown at any time, in rows or broadcast. It thrives better in the shade than in the sun. Soil mixed with compost is the preferred soil for this crop. Mulch attaches to the leaf and is difficult to detach. This plant requires frequent watering. Chervil is harvested by cutting it close to the ground and bundling it for sale. It is a seasoning plant. The seed is harvested from plants sown in September, which come up in spring and bear fruit in June or July. The seed lasts two years. Bulbous chervil differs from bulbous chervil in that its taproot is similar to that of the carrot, but gray in color. It is native to the region, as is bulbous chervil, and is broadcast in September or October for harvest in June or July. It is also sown in February and March for harvesting in August and September. Sowing requires amended soil and a bed of compost. Keep the soil moist until the seed has emerged. The roots are harvested and eaten like chestnuts, with the same flavor, and stored like potatoes. The seed is harvested by replanting roots in spring in good exposure, giving the carriers frequent wetting in the early stages to facilitate recovery, and harvesting the seed in July. The seed lasts for two years. Edible mushroom. See Special crops below. Wild chicory. Chicory is a native compound that market gardeners only grow in hot climates. It is sown on a bed in January or February, broadcast and a little thickly. The young leaves are cut as soon as they are sufficiently developed and before they harden. This is done several times, as long as the product is profitable, and then the bed is ruined. Seedlings are sown in the open ground in spring, producing a harvest of leaves until autumn. At this time, the plant is covered with a good bed of potting soil, into which it grows etiolated leaves. These leaves are cut between two plots of land, and sold under the name of white chicory. To make chicory known as capuchin beard, sow wild chicory in April in rays spaced 15 to 20 centimeters apart. In October, the plant and its roots are removed with a fork and placed in gauges. A layer of well-mixed manure is then prepared in a cellar or cellar, sheltered from light. As soon as it warms up, we place the chicory roots in bunches, taking care to remove the bad leaves. Water the plants lightly until vegetation develops, then only when the leaves start to droop, as this would cause the plant to rot internally. It takes fifteen to eighteen days to produce a harvest. If you take care to maintain the layer of new manure to keep it warm, you can continue the same work on the rest of the chicory, which you have put in a gauge until March and April. In the North, we grow a variety with large roots which, when burned and ground, are used to make chicory coffee. This is not the subject of this article. The seed is harvested from the previous year's plants in August. The heads must be rubbed by hand to separate the seed. In large-scale cultivation, the heads are beaten with a flail. The seed lasts for eight years. This compound originated in the tropical regions of the Old World and probably in the East Indies. - The varieties grown by market gardeners are first and foremost Rouen chicory, followed by Italian, Meaux and escarole. Chicory is sown in January, February and March, on a thick layer of mixed manure covered with boxes and frames. When the heat developed by the manure is so great that it is hard for the hand to bear, the seed is sown on the compost without covering it. It should germinate within twenty-four hours. As soon as you see that it has sprouted, you sprinkle the seedling with fine potting soil, while continuing to keep the layer at the same temperature. A layer is then prepared for transplanting, on which 400 or 500 plants per frame are placed. This layer is kept well covered against frost overnight. A fortnight or three weeks later, the chicory can be planted out. Give it air whenever the temperature permits. It can be combined with new tomatoes in trays, or with other chicories that have half come into season. To plant cold chicory, you need to get the plant used to the outside temperature by giving it frequent airing. Twenty-five or thirty chicories are planted per frame and three per bell. Tomatoes are used as counterplants, with six or nine per frame and one per bell. Cold planting should not take place before April, otherwise the plant will be prone to bolting. Tomatoes should only be counter-planted when chicory is half established. If you sow chicory in the ground in September, you can plant it under a frame and harvest it in winter, taking care to protect this cold-weather plant. All seedlings made before June should be sown in the soil, but it is no longer necessary to hasten germination from April onwards. Sow in the ground from June onwards. Plants can be planted in the open ground from May onwards, but the plant must be accustomed to the outside temperature. From May 15 onwards, all chicory seedlings, transplants and plantings can be exposed to the air: so remove the frames and their cases. In June, white or Turkish celery chicory is counter-planted in fields where apple cabbage and onions have been grown, as soon as the harvest of these vegetables has been completed. The cultivation of escarole is no less important than that of the previous crop, but takes place in autumn. If you want to harvest it early, sow it hot. It is grown in the open ground in the same way as chicory. Its disadvantages lie in beds of lettuce, romaine, celeriac and cauliflower. Depending on the time of sowing, it is harvested in September, October and November. Curly endive and escarole are eaten as salads; as they are only valued when the leaves are tender and white, they need to be etiolated. This is done in the following way: As soon as the vines have developed normally and the plant is in full bloom and the leaves have spread out well, we place a tie of rye straw under the leaves, joining them at the top, and bind them slightly so as to prevent the light from penetrating to the heart of the plant. As soon as you see that the plant is white inside, pull it out. It has been suggested that the stem be removed under an upside-down flowerpot, in which the chicory plant is enclosed. This is more or less successful in dry weather, but the plant rots in wet weather. In any case, only chicory and escarole that have been bound and cleaned, are white inside and have green leaves, are sought after at the market. To harvest from seed, we put beautiful plants under frames to protect them from frost. In March, each clump is removed and planted in a good spot. Care should be taken to separate curly endive and escarole plants, as hybridization is easy and produces no useful products. The seed is stored on the stalks, which are bunched together; these stalks are beaten as and when needed, as mice are fond of it when it is separated from its envelopes, and don't attack it otherwise because the bunches are hung from the ceiling; preserved in this way, the seed can be used for seven or eight years. You can also sow the seedbeds in January and February for planting in March, but you can't be sure of success or of getting such clean seed, because in the first case you know which plant will bear fruit, and in the second you don't know what you'll get. Cabbage. Plant of the cruciferous family, native to Western Europe. Two species are grown: apple cabbage and cauliflower. Peony cabbage itself is subdivided into a large number of cultivated varieties, the main ones being York, beef, Bonneuil and red cabbage. The cabbages to be harvested in spring are sown towards the end of August. This makes SaintFiacre a memorable date for cabbage cultivation. By September 10 at the latest, you should stop sowing spring cabbages. You need to know the age of the seed, because if you sow a young seed too early, the plant will bolt in the spring. Cabbages are transplanted in October in beds, and the final planting takes place in November and December in deeply tilled, well-smoked soil. Early cabbages are planted 35 to 40 centimetres apart, larger ones 45 to 50 centimetres apart. Planting takes place in rays, which you trace as deeply as possible with your foot. In spring, hoe the soil well and fold it back into the rays. Spinach or baby lettuce is then sown between the cabbage rows. This is done in April. Early cabbages are harvested from May 15 to June 15, and the others from June 15 to the end of July. Bonneuil cabbage is sown in January or February under cloches and is also transplanted under cloches. It is planted in April and in May, succeeding the cœur-de-bœuf. There are two varieties of red cabbage: large and small. The latter is preferred for its earlier maturity. It is sown in May or April, planted in May or June, and harvested in September or October. For information on growing Quental, Milan, Vertus, Brussels and kohlrabi cabbages, etc., please refer to the chapter on agricultural crops. Everyone knows how to use choupomme. For seed harvesting, we select the best cabbages and mark them, then cut off the head, leaving four or five leaves on the stem. Buds soon develop on this stem, and the root ball is transplanted to a separate spot. Each eye thus developed can be removed by splitting the stem and planting the eye with the portion of stem to which it is attached. When each eye has taken root, it is set in place and protected from frost over the winter. In spring, the flowering stem sprouts, and the siliques are harvested before maturity and dried in the shade. When the silique dries on the ground, it pops open and the seeds are dispersed. The small pod containing the seeds is called a silique. Seeds can still be harvested from cabbages sown in May. This is one of the plants most prone to hybridization, so keep seedbeds away not only from other cabbage varieties, but also from other crucifers such as turnips, mustard, turnips, radishes, rapeseed, etc. Cauliflower. Cauliflower - This cruciferous plant differs from the previous one in that its flowering parts are sufficiently large and fleshy before their full development to be the main focus of cultivation and provide succulent food. Cauliflowers are divided into three varieties: tender cauliflower, semi-hard cauliflower and hard cauliflower. For tender cauliflower, we grow Petit Salomon and Lenormand cauliflower in spring. They are sown on beds from September 1st when grown in hot conditions; if grown in cold conditions, they are sown on the ground from the 10th to the 15th. In October, 200 to 300 plants per frame are transplanted under cold frames, in a thick layer of potting soil. The plant is watered to bind it immediately after transplanting. In December, the plant is pulled out and ploughed thoroughly before being put back in with a good layer of soil. Air is given when it doesn't freeze, but at the slightest sign of frost, cover with mats or litter. Cauliflower is planted on a bed in January or February. If the bed contains no carrots, plant six plants per frame. If, on the other hand, the bed contains carrots, the number is reduced to four cauliflowers. Other growers plant cauliflowers on old beds and counterplant with Georges or Gotte lettuces, depending on their own ideas. When the cauliflowers are getting stronger, the frames are removed as soon as the plants have become too strong for the shelters; but, if frosts are still to be feared, stakes and string are stretched out to shelter the cauliflowers during the night by means of straw mats laid over the stakes and string. Full-ground cultivation takes place when four cauliflower plants per cold frame have been planted in lettuces and romaine. In March, cauliflowers can be planted in a square or as a counterplant in other plants, among carrot and radish seedlings. All these cauliflowers, sown in September, are those of the first spring season. They are also planted on melon trenches and on compost in July. The semi-hard variety is sown in cloches in November, transplanted in trays or cloches in December and planted in April. 1 Sow month by month from January to June. This plant requires a lot of care. As soon as the bud appears, it must be covered, which is done by pulling off a leaf from the mother plant and covering the indiscreet flower. This care must be repeated every day, as it's the whiteness of the cauliflower head that's important. Water frequently, at least every other day. Caterpillars, slugs and earwigs are formidable enemies of this vegetable. When the flower's granulations start to spread, it's time to pick the cauliflower. September seedlings, planted warm, produce a harvest in May, and in June if planted cold. The same seedlings, planted in the ground, only yield in July. Cauliflowers sown at St. Theresa's Day are harvested in July. January and February seedlings are harvested in August, April and May in September and October, June in November and December. This last harvest is often thwarted by frost. In this case, we pull up the plants with their clods and put them in gauges under frames covered with straw mats or manure; we stop sowing from June 15th to start again in September. The tender variety is harvested from the earliest and most beautiful plants. The semi-hard variety is harvested mid-harvest in the same way, and the hard variety at the end of the season. Seed-bearing plants are left to grow, and watered moderately and at the base: excess humidity encourages the development of aphids and plaster. Harvest before maturity and dry the heads hanging in the shade. This seed lasts five years. Broccoli. - Native to Italy. It differs from cauliflower only in its violet hue. It produces eyelets like Brussels sprouts. It has the same uses as cauliflower. It is sown from March to May, then planted 50 to 60 centimetres apart. It is harvested in September and throughout the winter. Although more robust than cauliflower, it is useful to put it in a gauge under a frame when frosts are severe. Crambe cabbage. (See Agricultural crops.) - The leaves of this type of cabbage are eaten blanched like asparagus. If you want to force them, do exactly as you would for forced asparagus. Do not give the frames any light. Ciboule. A lily native to northern Asia. You start sowing chives in February to have them all year round, and stop sowing in October. Lamb's lettuce is combined with September sowings; for other sowings, radishes are sown, or romaine and lettuce are planted. Sow thickly if you don't need to put anything in the seedbed. This plant needs plenty of water and a thick bed of compost. It can be stored over winter in a gauge covered with straw mats or bedding. Chives do not set seed until the second year. Harvesting takes place in August. The seed lasts two years. Perennial chives last five or six years in place; they are propagated by their bulbs. Chives are used as a seasoning plant. Civet. A lily native to southern Europe, the civet is planted in small clumps whose bulbs multiply rapidly. By dividing the clumps when they are too strong and planting the parts, it can be multiplied ad infinitum. Plant in borders or well-smoked beds, in rays spaced 15 to 20 centimetres apart. Leave the civet in the same spot for three or four years, covering it with a good bed of compost over winter. This plant requires a lot of water. It is cut five or six times a year. Before relocating the civet, the new bed should be well smoked. A few clumps are planted on beds in spring for early harvests. Civets are used for the same purposes as spring onions. In many countries, this plant is known as an appetizer, no doubt because of its ability to stimulate digestion. In this sense, the word is only used in the plural: civet sprigs are called appetites. Cucumber. A cucurbit native to Asia Minor and Western Europe. It is mainly grown as an early variety with white fruit, long green fruit and small green fruit, or gherkin. Cucumbers are sown on a warm bed, with 150 plants per frame. A third layer is prepared a fortnight after transplanting; it should be 60 centimetres thick, with mixed manure and a 20-centimetre bed of potting soil. Two or three plants per frame are planted, after topping and retaining only two eyes. Planting is done by hand and in clods, to facilitate recovery. The soil is covered with a light mulch to divide the water for frequent watering. Shade the frames when the sun is too hot, provide frequent airing and only uncover when the plants are vigorous enough to fill the frames, and then only if the weather permits. As the plants are topped at two eyes, only two branches remain on each plant, which are left to spin and spread out. Two types of flowers appear successively on the same plant: male flowers bearing the stamens, and female flowers bearing the pistil. Only the latter bear fruit. The latter, called meshes, are fertilized by pollen from the former. Fertilization has taken place as soon as the fruit has set, i.e. when the swelling or ovary of the mesh increases in volume at the same time as the corolla withers and falls. Only one or two eyes are left above each new-born fruit. Harvesting begins in May and June. The change in fruit color indicates maturity. Cultivation under cloche is done with seedlings from later sowings. Transplanting takes place on an old bed, which is turned over and warmed with small quantities of new manure. A 20-meter layer can accommodate thirty-five or forty bells at most. A foot is planted under each bell. The work is the same as for planting under a frame. The cloches are removed at the same time as the trays. Cucumbers can be eaten as salads, or cooked and stuffed. Gherkin. - This is a variety of green cucumber with rough fruit covered with calluses and furrowed with wrinkles. It is hot-sown in April and May, and is treated in exactly the same way as its congeners, right up to planting, which is done on old, unchanged beds in well-exposed costières. It needs sun, a good mulch and some watering. Gherkins are not pruned. The fruit is harvested before maturity and sold to vinegar makers. Harvesting takes place every other day in July or August. The seeds of all cucumber varieties are harvested from an elongated, ripe fruit. The fruit is cut and the seeds removed, dried and stored for up to five years. As hybridization is easy, different varieties should not be grown in close proximity to each other. Squash. Squash is a type of cucurbit native to India. There are several varieties, xmlns="http://www.loc.gov/standards/alto/ns-v4#" xsi:schemaLocation="http://www.loc.gov/standards/alto/ns-v4# http://www.loc.gov/standards/alto/v4/alto-4-3.xsd">
