VPOS="3924" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="157" GC="1.0"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2274 3924 2274 4081 2274 4081 2274 3924"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1095" CONTENT="a" HPOS="2325" VPOS="3931" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="168" GC="1.0"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2325 3931 2325 4099 2325 4099 2325 3931"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1096" CONTENT="g" HPOS="2376" VPOS="3941" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="147" GC="1.0"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2376 3941 2376 4088 2376 4088 2376 3941"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1097" CONTENT="e" HPOS="2427" VPOS="3941" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="162" GC="0.9995"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2427 3941 2427 4103 2427 4103 2427 3941"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1098" CONTENT="s" HPOS="2478" VPOS="3941" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="163" GC="1.0"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2478 3941 2478 4104 2478 4104 2478 3941"/> </Shape> </Glyph> </String>             <SP ID="segment_384" HPOS="2554" VPOS="3941" WIDTH="13" HEIGHT="160"/> <String ID="segment_385" CONTENT="fré¬" HPOS="2593" VPOS="3920" WIDTH="127" HEIGHT="179" WC="0.9988"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2593 3935 2593 4099 2720 4094 2720 3937"/> </Shape> <Glyph ID="char_1100" CONTENT="f" HPOS="2593" VPOS="3935" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="164" GC="0.9997"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2593 3935 2593 4099 2593 4099 2593 3935"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1101" CONTENT="r" HPOS="2631" VPOS="3927" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="171" GC="0.9999"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2631 3927 2631 4098 2631 4098 2631 3927"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1102" CONTENT="e" HPOS="2669" VPOS="3920" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="176" GC="0.9997"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2669 3920 2669 4096 2669 4096 2669 3920"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1103" CONTENT="́" HPOS="2682" VPOS="3921" WIDTH="13" HEIGHT="175" GC="1.0"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2682 3921 2682 4096 2695 4095 2695 3926"/> </Shape> </Glyph> <Glyph ID="char_1104" CONTENT="¬" HPOS="2720" VPOS="3937" WIDTH="0" HEIGHT="157" GC="0.9947"> <Shape> <Polygon POINTS="2720 3937 2720 4094 2720 4094 2720 3937"/> </Shape> </Glyph> </String> </TextLine> </TextBlock> </PrintSpace> </Page> </Layout> </alto> Pear is a chenopod native to the Alps. Two varieties are grown: poirée blonde and poirée à cardes. Poirée blonde can be sown from March to July in rows or broadcast. It can be thinned out if sown too thickly, which is detrimental to leaf development. It likes water and well-smoked soil, and requires good topdressing when sowing. It can be warmed in winter in the same way as sorrel. Sow in June in a nursery. As soon as it has developed, it is counter-planted 30 to 40 centimetres apart in other half-planted plants. It requires a lot of water. In winter, we cover it with manure, and in spring, we uncover it and remove the spoiled leaves. It is also mounded like artichokes, according to the method used around Lyon. Ordinary pear plants are harvested two or three times, leaf by leaf, and then stored for the following spring, with a good covering of compost for winter. This leaf is used to sweeten sorrel. Chard is harvested the year after planting, in April and May, and used in the same way as cardoons. The seed is harvested from second-year plants that are left to grow in the spring and harvested in July and August; this seed lasts five years. Cultivated pea. See Agricultural crops. Potatoes. The potato is a solanaceous plant native to America. Market gardeners only cultivate this plant on hot beds, as an early crop. The variety of choice is the earlier-ripening marjoram. Tubers chosen for sowing must be of medium size and well differentiated. They are planted in January at a rate of nine to twelve tillers per frame in three rows. As soon as the leaves have developed, the plant is ridged and harvesting begins in late March or April. To harvest the potatoes, we dig the mounds by hand without pulling out the stalks, and we take care to cover any that are not at their full size. The plantation is ruined before full maturity, as this species, grown in this way, can be eaten before the tubers have fully expanded. Frames should be covered when frost is feared; air should be provided as often as possible, and water in small quantities if the drought is too severe. Market gardeners who have a greenhouse can put some in pots, and obtain them free of charge. For open-ground potatoes that market gardeners don't grow, see the Agricultural Crops chapter. 1i This is a portulaceous plant native to America, of which two varieties are grown: green purslane and golden purslane. Purslane is sown on a bed in January, but on its own. It is cut with a knife, and if the bed is immediately cleaned and the paths reworked, a second harvest can be obtained immediately. Sow in the ground from March to August; in summer, sow in the shade. A few seeds can be sown in the ground with other water-loving plants, but must be pulled up before it interferes with other plants. It's always best to sow it alone. After each sowing, cover with potting soil. As the seed is very fine, we mix it with fine compost to avoid sowing too thickly. We let spring-sown seedlings go to seed. Harvest a little before maturity, as the fine seed could be lost. This seed lasts eight years. This plant can be eaten as a salad or cooked. Raadis. The radish is an indigenous cruciferous plant. The preferred varieties are: the scarlet red radish, which is the best of all, because it doesn't take on leaves like other radishes during hot cultivation; on soil, it digs in less quickly and gives more time for harvesting; during the summer, the white-tipped radish is preferred. We still grow the early pink half-long and long radish, the white half-long and long tip, the round purple, the early yellow and the white radish. Turnips, which differ from radishes only in their more elongated roots and are divided into white, early purple and pink, and black radishes are also grown by market gardeners. Radishes can be sown all year round; their seeds are combined with other seeds; sometimes they are sown on their own, in rays in summer, or in ados in spring and autumn. In spring, those that have been sown overados should be covered with straw mats. The first sowings take place in January and the last on October 9. The boxes are placed first, the seed is sown in them and, as soon as the frosts appear, the frames are placed and covered with straw mats if the frosts are severe. These radishes are sold while waiting for the new ones, which are sown in layers in December and January for harvesting in February and March. We sow them in carrots and take care to give them a little air when they start to develop. In January and February, carrot seedlings continue to be sown in trays, cloches and in the air. Give them as much air as you can without danger, and only cover them if it freezes. On airy beds, radishes are covered flat in frosty weather, but if there are plants to be secured like lettuces, a trellis is set up to support the mat. Otherwise, this precaution is not necessary, as radishes are not afraid of being trodden on by straw mats. In late January and February, radishes are sown in the ground on the ridge and on the counter-ridge. In March, they are sown in the open, always in other seeds, and successively throughout the summer. Radishes require a lot of potting soil; the thicker the layer, the longer and more beautiful they become. If radishes are to be sown on their own, they should be sown in rows. Radishes need a lot of water. In summer, harvest twenty or twenty-five days after sowing. Black radish. - This plant is broadcast in June in association with other radishes, and can be planted with romaine or lettuce. As soon as the seed has emerged and the radishes are starting to develop, they are thinned out to give those left enough room to grow. They are harvested in autumn. As radishes are sold all winter long, those that are only sold at this time are put in gauges after the tops have been cut off. They must be covered with bedding in frosty weather. They are sold in bunches or by the dozen. Two varieties are grown: violet de Gournay and black. Like all crucifers, radishes are very unhealthy. Seeds are harvested from plants sown in the spring and transplanted into prepared soil, 20 to 25 centimetres apart in all directions. They grow rapidly, and when the seeds are fully developed, we pull them out and dry them, either by hanging them from poles or trellises, or by leaving them on the ground, taking care to turn them over. The stalks are beaten to isolate the seed, which lasts for five years. Horseradish. See Agricultural crops. Rapunzel. Rapunzel is a native campanulaceae. It is broadcast in June and July. The seed should be mixed with a little dry soil or compost, as it is very fine. You can combine the seed with carrot seed and plant romaine or lettuce in the seedbed, but if you sow it with radishes or spinach, don't plant it. Give it a light mulch and plenty of watering when it sprouts, so that the heat doesn't burn it. Harvesting begins in February or March and continues throughout the winter; the plant is pulled up with its root, as the root and leaf are edible and eaten in salads with lamb's lettuce. This plant doesn't need to be protected in winter, as it doesn't fear frost. Riponce seeds are harvested in the year following sowing; a few are left to rise in a piece of planking, the least attractive being removed, and the seed is harvested in July; it lasts five years Rhubarb is a polygonaceous plant native to Asia. This plant is rarely grown by Parisian market gardeners, but almost all of those in London cultivate it. It is sown in June, or propagated in spring by separating plants. Each plant is spaced one meter apart. The plantation is hoeed in spring, the weeds and bad leaves are cut off, and the petioles are harvested in May, the year after sowing. They are used as a condiment in some dishes and pastries. Two varieties are grown: wavy rhubarb and Nepaul rhubarb; the latter is preferred. The seed lasts four years and is harvested from the best plants of each variety. White Salsisis and Scorsonère. See Agricultural crops. A labiate native to southern Europe, savory is sown in spring in a reserved spot, where it reproduces itself in subsequent years without requiring any care. Used to season sauces. Tetra one. New Zealand spinach is a ficoid native to Oceania. It is sown in March in layers, after soaking the seeds because of their thick skin. As soon as frost is no longer a concern, plant tetragonia in light, well-tilled soil, 40 to 50 centimetres apart. They can be sown on site in April. When the stems cover the ground, cut off the leaves at the tips of the young shoots. It's these leaves that are used as 1. spinach. It's more of a vegetable than a market-garden plant. To harvest the seed, plant a few stalks in a corner of the garden. It is dried in the shade. It lasts for five years. Tomato. The tomato is a solanaceous plant native to South America. It can be grown in hot pots from November in pineapple greenhouses, but market gardening is done in beds or trenches. Sowing takes place in January under warm conditions, as does transplanting. Between 200 and 300 plants are placed in each frame, which are then planted in March and April in a warm environment on a bed or trench 1.33 m wide, with frames or cloches. Nine tomatoes are planted under each frame, 40 centimetres apart, but only one per bell, leaving 46 centimetres between each plant. Two or three rows of bells are planted, but if only two rows are planted, the trench should be reduced to 1 meter wide and 30 to 35 centimeters thick. The tomato is given air as soon as it is picked up. If you don't want to cover it with mats, it's essential to do so; however, you should cover it in the early stages to speed up recovery. This plant is very voracious and can be carried away in wood; it needs a lot of air, but is very sensitive to frost: these precautions require assiduous care. For the first method, tomatoes require topping, after which three or four stems are left to grow from the base. Care should be taken to remove any eyes that appear on the stems in the leaf axils, and as soon as the bunch has developed, it can be stopped immediately if you are sure of the temperature. But most of the time, as we're still afraid of the cold, which could cause the first flowers to abort, we leave a second bunch to develop to avoid any accidents that could happen to the first. In this way, six bunches will have developed instead of three, if the temperature remains favorable, since the two left behind will bear fruit. It's true that leaving only one bunch per stem will result in a slightly earlier harvest, but with six bunches you'll have almost a double crop. The six bunches are placed on two rows of wire, the lower supporting the first bunches and the upper the second bunches. In the second method, the tomato is not topped, but only two stems are left with the head, on which two bunches are allowed to develop, and, with the head forming a central bunch, there are five bunches per plant. This process produces fruit for sale a little earlier than the previous one; as the head bunch absorbs almost all the sap, the lateral stems develop only slowly and produce little fruit, whereas in the previous process, the stems all develop equally and the fruit sets almost all at the same time. Market gardeners who use the second method only sell a small quantity of tomatoes harvested from the top bunches, eight days earlier than other market gardeners, and only sell the rest three weeks later. This plant requires great care. All shoots should be removed as soon as the bunches are well tied. If the weather is hot, water abundantly, even when in full bloom; but if it's humid, even without much rain, stop watering. Cold damp weather makes tomatoes sick, but it's essential to keep them wet during hot weather. Harvesting takes place in July and August. To trellis tomatoes, plant a good stake every four feet in each row. Galvanized wire (No. 9 or 10) is stretched and tied to the first stake, then to the second stake, making a turn after tightening the wire, and from there to the third, doing the same, and so on to the end of the row. Care is taken not to cut this wire, which will still be used for the same purpose in subsequent years, but to wrap it around the stake to raise the second row to the required height, and so on to the end. Instead of doing all three rows at once, wait a few days after doing the first row before doing the second, which is then placed at the same height as the heads, making for more regular trellising. This trellising is done with good rye straw. This work makes it easier to care for the plant; the fruit develops more quickly, and the harvest ripens faster and better. Hot tomatoes are also planted in layers of chicory, half of which is hot. Field-grown tomatoes are sown hot in February and March and transplanted hot in the same way. The plants require the same care and labor as the first tomatoes. We believe there is a benefit in topping, but there is less inconvenience in not topping than with the first crop. Tomatoes must also be trellised and leaf buds removed. Tomatoes are also grown under cold frames and cloches; they can be planted in half or even three-quarters-ripened chicory, but experience has shown that it is preferable to plant them on their own in both hot and cold conditions. Among cultivated varieties, it's the small, early variety that's planted in the hot season; the medium-sized variety is planted in both the hot and cold seasons; the large variety is planted in pots. Tomatoes are planted in the ground, 50 to 60 centimeters apart, in three rows, in a standard bed 2.35 meters wide; for this purpose, a good exposure is always chosen, either on a ridge or a counter-bed. You can start planting as early as May 15th, but you risk losing the planting if there is a late frost, which happens all too often. Under frames and cold cloches, planting can start as early as the first half of April. Field tomatoes can be tied around a sturdy stake rather than trellised, although the latter method is preferable. Cold harvesting follows hot harvesting in August and September, and ground harvesting takes place in September and October. Care is the same for all. As soon as the fruit begins to redden, the leaves can be removed, provided the sun is not too hot. The seed is harvested by picking the earliest and best-formed fruits each season, as soon as they are fully red. They are left to ripen in the shade and in the open air. Then we open them and drop the seed and pulp into a bucket full of water. - The seed is then collected in a colander, with the pulp carefully separated, and the cleaned seed falls to the bottom of the bucket. After changing the water several times, the seed is collected and dried as quickly as possible, as it germinates easily if it retains a little moisture. It should therefore be stirred every day, or spread out very widely on a cloth. - Tomato seeds germinate over a period of five years. The tomato fruit is a highly prized edible whose pulp is used as a condiment in a host of culinary preparations. Thyme. A labiate plant native to southern Europe, thyme is sown in spring, but is generally propagated by separating its large clumps into smaller ones, which are usually planted in rows or borders. The seed is harvested a little before maturity and dried in the shade, lasting three years. Thyme branches are used as seasoning in sauces. Seed-bearing plants of the same family and of different varieties should not be placed close together, as hybridization could easily occur through the effect of the wind or the travels of butterflies and bees. Almost all seed harvesting takes place in July and August. Only squeeze the seeds when they are completely dry, seal them in bags with labels, keep them dry and out of the reach of mice, which are very fond of them. SPECIAL CROPS. We have grouped together under this heading the crops that some horticulturists have made a specialty of, either because they require special equipment, as in the case of pineapples, strawberries and forced asparagus; or because they require special conditions in the building where they are grown, as in the case of mushroom and watercress plants. The cultivation of these plants for the hall trade would be unprofitable if done on a small scale; market gardeners therefore have no interest in indulging in it; it is found, apart from specialties, in the kitchen gardens of large houses, at least for certain plants, and it is with the aim of being useful to the gardeners of these large houses that we are writing this chapter. We will deal here with the cultivation of pineapple, edible mushroom, watercress and thermosiphon forced strawberry. Pineapple. The pineapple is a bromeliad native to South America, whose fragrant fruit is much sought-after on opulent tables. The high price of these cone-shaped fruits, which are comparable to pine cones, has led some horticulturists to specialize in this crop, despite the assiduous care it requires. Pineapples are generally propagated by eyelet. - Sowing is a slow process, but it is sometimes rewarded with the creation of a new variety. Carnations are planted in the first fortnight of October. They are best taken from the leaf axils, where they are always strongest; only keep the old plant if you're short of seedlings. Before planting, remove 5 or 6 centimetres of the leaves from the part of the plant to be planted, and after this cut has been made cleanly and clearly, place each eyelet in a 10 to 12-centimetre diameter pot in heather soil. You can also multiply pineapples by crowns, in the same way, and even keep them for a month in the shade in a dry place, to be used if the need arises. Towards the end of September, a 60-centimeter-high layer of mixed manure, half new and half old, is applied. If the latter is not available, leaves can be used; this is covered with 20 to 30 centimetres of tannin and the pots are placed on top, spaced according to their strength and as close as possible to the glass. The frames are covered with straw mats overnight. As soon as vegetation starts to appear, air is supplied in small quantities and from the opposite side to the wind; the pineapples are watered at the base when they need it, taking care that the water does not penetrate into the horns of the leaves. Warm up the bed with hot manure whenever necessary. In spring, we multiply the watering, as this plant only fears humidity during the winter; we put the pots on another layer where the tannin has been replaced by soil. This layer should be of medium strength, given the temperature of the season. The plants are taken out of the pot to inspect the roots and remove any rotten ones; a few leaves are removed from the bottom, then replanted in clods on the new layer, where they remain until September. Continue to provide frequent air and watering. In September, the roots are examined again, as they renew themselves every year; the pineapples are tied with a straw tie to facilitate repotting, which takes place in 24-centimetre pots. They are then placed back on the bed and cared for in the same way as carnations, until they put down roots. In January, they are returned to the greenhouse, where a thick layer of earth or moss has been prepared, so that the pots can be easily hidden and placed 50 centimetres apart. The pineapples are left in this position until the fruits appear. In July, after reshuffling the layer, we replace the tan or moss we've used with soil. Once the pineapples have been placed in the greenhouse, instead of making a layer, the pots can be buried in tannin or moss and placed on a tarpaulin, under which a thermosiphon circulates. The heating should be regulated so as not to exceed 25 to 30 degrees centigrade, whatever the season. The water used to water the plants must also be at greenhouse temperature. Basins are also useful. Water laden with animal or plant detritus is preferable to pure water for watering at the foot. As second-season plants need less heat than first-season plants, the pineapple greenhouse is usually divided into two parts by a glass partition. First-season fruit ripen from July to September. Second-season fruit, obtained from plants where the temperature has been maintained at 12 degrees until March, when they receive the same care as the others, are harvested from September to December. The Cayenne, Martinique and Providence pineapples are among the most popular varieties, while the Mont-Serrat, Enville and Comte de Paris varieties are among those obtained from seed. It takes two years to produce a few pineapples, and it's easy to see how much material, fertilizer and manpower this small amount of fruit required. It's not surprising, then, that they continue to fetch a very high price, far higher than that of the pineapples from the West Indies that steamers now bring rapidly to our ports and that the railroads bring to Paris. These latter fruits, cut before they are fully ripe, do not retain their bouquet and are far inferior in flavor to the products of the greenhouses of our skilled specialists. Edible mushroom. The edible mushroom is an acotyledonous plant of the agaric family. It grows spontaneously in meadows, in crops and mainly in diaper debris, which has earned it the name "diaper mushroom". This plant does not reproduce from seed, and the sporules it leaves to the wind in its natural state cannot be collected or preserved. Before any cultivation, it is necessary to obtain a greyish-white, mould-like material called "blanc de champignon", which generally develops during the fermentation of horse manure, and which is entwined in this manure by numerous, intertwined fibers and radicles. Recent observations tend to prove that bran and oats are necessary for the 12 feeding of animals in whose manure the white fungus develops. It would also seem useful for this manure to come from male animals. In any case, spawn is distinguished from mold by its pronounced mushroom odor. It is collected and stored in a dry place. Mushrooms are grown either in the open air, or in underground quarries or cellars with low humidity. To grow in the open air, unload a wagon or two of good whole horse manure, fork it over and wet it. This manure is then wetted with a pitchfork, and mixed twice more at eight-day intervals, wetting only if the manure is too dry. Before using this manure, it must be well made and have lost none of its heat. The land is then prepared by ploughing, and the manure is laid out in 60-centimeter-wide stacks, with a path of equal width between each. A layer of manure 60 centimetres wide at the base and 8 or 10 centimetres at the top, over an indefinite length, is called a millstone. To make the grindstone, the manure is placed on the base width, piled up with the hands and squeezed with the feet and knees; the finished grindstone has the shape of a 60 to 70-centimeter-high speed bump. Once the wheel has been completed, the manure is left to ferment for a further eight days, then a pinch of mushroom spawn is introduced on each side and in the middle of the body of the wheel. This work is called larding the wheel. The pinch of spawn thus introduced is called a mise. Each bet should be spaced 20 to 25 centimetres apart. When you see that the white is developing, you plow the bottom of the paths, between the millstones, and bring in good free soil. When you don't have enough, you use small wooden shovels to cover the millstones with earth to a thickness of 2 or 3 centimetres. This is called gobeting the millstones, and then you cover them with a layer of litter called a chemise, and put 2 or 3 centimetres of manure on top. The next steps are: 1° in the event of frost, to cover the millstones with manure or straw mats; 2° during mushroom picking, to keep the millstones warm by occasionally placing a few hottées of good manure in the paths. Picking is done every other day, whatever the weather. You take a smooth, sharp fork, so as not to remove the liner from the millstone; you put the manure on either side of you; then you take two mannes, one empty to receive the harvest, the other full of clean earth to fill in the holes of the mushrooms you've picked and sprinkle over those that have just sprouted. To pick the mushrooms, you take the shirt from the millstone with both hands, turn it over on your knees, pick the mushrooms, fill in each hole and carefully replace the shirt. Once the mushrooms have been picked, the liner is covered with manure, and if the millstone needs heat, a small quantity of hot manure is brought in and placed only in the foot of the millstone. If the millstone is too dry, two or three watering cans are poured over the liner before replacing the manure. The fungus fears excess drought, but is no less afraid of excess humidity. Mushrooms can only be grown in the open air from October to December, as the electrical state of the air seems to have an unfortunate influence on their cultivation. To grow mushrooms in a cellar, the moulds are prepared in the same way as for cultivation in the open air; however, they are not covered with shirt or manure. All exits are sealed to intercept light and prevent the outside temperature from penetrating, as this is the key to the mushroom's whiteness. The same precautions are unnecessary in the quarries. The great depth of these 12 underground quarries means that they maintain a uniform temperature and that the electricity of the outside air has no effect below the 40 to 50 meters separating them from ground level. As a result, mushrooms can be grown in any season. Mushroom growers are convinced that syphilis exerts an unfortunate influence on the mushroom when it comes into contact with people suffering from this disease. Nor do they allow women near or in contact with their millstones. They support these beliefs with observations that may be poorly made. It would be useful for a scientific commission to study their value. We have already spoken of the influence of syphilis on the cultivation of melons; we have often heard these beliefs, which are based on fact, treated as absurd without proof. If a commission wanted to prove the absurdity of these beliefs, we would be happy to provide them with a few melons that could be touched by patients from the Hôpital du Midi, on condition that we would be compensated if the harvest was lost. The white that develops spontaneously in trenches or in manure is called blanc vierge. Mushrooms are prized as a delicacy and seasoning. A conscript who had taken refuge in a quarry and was trying to grow some vegetables is credited with discovering that mushroom spawn can grow in the dark and at a constant temperature. Open-air cultivation was practiced as early as the 16th century. Watercress. Watercress is a native cruciferous plant that grows spontaneously in shallow watercourses near the springs that feed them. As this plant has depurative properties and, at the same time, a pleasant, aperitive flavor that makes it highly sought-after as a food and seasoning, spring water has been used to cultivate it, which began around Senlis, on the banks of the Nonnette, at the beginning of this century. The land where watercress is grown is called a créssonnière. It is located below the spring that feeds it. A series of interconnected pits or ditches is dug across the slope, and the flow of water is regulated by valves set up at the points of communication. Once the ditches have been prepared and communication with the springs has been interrupted by a first valve, a seedling of watercress is planted in the spring at the bottom of each ditch, which has been thoroughly cleaned and from which any aquatic plant that might hinder the development of the watercress has been removed. Instead of seedlings, cuttings taken in August can be planted at the bottom of ditches, after running a trickle of water to moisten the soil. The cuttings are placed 12 to 15 centimetres apart. After a short time, the cuttings are rooted and the cress soon covers the soil. At this point, 12 to 18 centimetres of water are allowed to flow in, a sufficient quantity for this crop. If the plant has been well cared for, well chosen and well cleaned of any parasitic weeds, once established the cress bed requires no further care other than precautions against frost. To this end, water is left to rise above the cress for as long as the frost lasts, but this water must be drained off as soon as the temperature eases, as this submersion tires the plant. During thaws and rainstorms, the mother spring must be given special drainage to prevent the pits from silting up, which would kill the cress. A discharge pipe with no connection to the watercress bed is therefore essential. It is useful to protect the pits from the cold and heat by planting hedges between the pits, but it is important to prevent leaves from falling into the pits, as they prevent the cress from growing. This can be achieved by securing them with a trellis or straw mat. Watercress can be harvested using a large plank laid across the ditch; it can be cut off with a serpette, but it is preferable to detach it with the toenail to avoid loosening the plant. During the summer, as long as the season is favorable, a pit can be cut every three weeks, but if the season is cold, wait two months before making a second cut. When the year's harvest is finished, the pit is dried out and a light layer of well-consumed cow manure is spread over the entire surface, then the cress is pushed back into the pit with a long-handled board. Two workers armed with this instrument walk along the edge of the pit and push back the feet of cress lifted during cutting. A watercress plantation can last a long time if it is carefully tended, but as soon as you realize that it is withering away, you need to think about renewing it. To this end, we pull up the watercress with its roots, lay it on the banks between the ditches, plough the pit, smoke it with well-consumed cow manure and replant the watercress as we did at the beginning. Some growers pull up the plant every year, have the mud removed from the ditch, smoke the bottom after a good ploughing and replant the old plant. Sandstone pipes 8 centimeters in diameter are used for both water intake and drainage. If you want good-quality cress, let them flow freely. Valves are used only to stop the water flowing in times of frost, and to divert it in times of thaw and storms. Watercress seed is harvested from the best plants in each pit in August. It lasts four years. In addition to its use as a food, watercress is the basis of all antiscorbutic preparations; used alone, it is enough to cure sailors of scurvy. It is grown in the Paris area, wherever it has been possible to use a spring or stream located at the base of a plot of land where a watercress bed can be established. Thermosiphon forced strawberries. After having grown strawberry seedlings as described in the chapter on Cultivation, instead of planting them in September in the ground, they are planted in 15-centimeter diameter pots filled with good soil, into which each strawberry plant is placed with its root ball. Boxes are made to hold the frames, and the pots are placed tightly together inside, covered to protect them from frost. They should be given a few basins to encourage recovery. In December or January, place a tier of four shelves above the thermosiphon pipes and cover them with tarpaulins lined with stretchers. These tarpaulins are secured with good supports on each side, and the pots are placed tightly together on this tier, after removing dead leaves and refreshing the soil. Cover at night with straw mats and maintain a temperature of 12 to 15 degrees under the tarpaulins. The plants are given frequent basins and air when the temperature permits. This costly method produces strawberries in March, whereas other methods only produce strawberries in April and May. Pineapple growers take advantage of the temperature in their greenhouses to harvest strawberries forced by this process. Potted strawberry plants can produce a second crop if they are put back in the ground and watered. Sweet potato. The sweet potato is a convolvulaceae native to tropical America. Although this plant requires no more care than the potato to thrive in the south of France, in the climate of Paris it becomes delicate and difficult, and can only be raised successfully by the following means. Multiply it by its starchy tubers, similar to those of the potato. Choose the healthiest ones and plant them on a warm bed covered with soil or compost. Boxes and frames are placed over this layer, and it is covered at night with straw mats. The eyes develop and soon reach 6 to 8 centimetres in length. At this point, they are removed and transplanted into 6-centimeter diameter pots, which are then buried in another layer. Each cutting is covered with a bell, given a few basins and air to firm up the plant when it is well established. A third layer of good soil mixed with compost is then prepared, and three potato plants per frame are planted, stoves are placed in the paths and mats are laid down overnight. Instead of potting the potato plant, you can put it in place straight away by cutting off part of the tuber with each bud, which will serve to nourish the plant and facilitate its recovery. As the tubers grow, they rise above the soil and need to be replenished with soil, air and frequent watering. Harvesting begins in May or June. The largest ones are removed, and the others are covered with soil. Vegetation lasts until autumn. In April, potatoes can be planted on old carrot beds, one per cloche, and covered with manure until they recover. Then remove the manure and give them some air. In May, we make square holes 40 to 50 centimetres square, 50 to 60 centimetres apart. These holes are 35 to 40 centimetres deep. Fill them with good manure, cover with a good thickness of soil and plant three potato vines in each hole. They are covered with a shaded cloche, and when the plants are well established, they are uncovered and left in the open air. Harvesting takes place in October; the tubers must be handled with care, as they rot easily if bruised or crumpled. To preserve them, let them dry carefully, then put them in a crate on a bed of tannin, cover the first bed with tannin and place a new layer of potatoes on top, and so on until the harvest is tight. If tannin is not available, dry soil can be used. These boxes should be kept in a warm, dry place. They can also be kept in place by covering them with frames or dry manure, to protect them from humidity and frost. This is probably the best way to preserve them, as the little vegetation they can retain tends to make them healthier. They can be stored in this way until February and March. This plant rarely produces ripe seeds, even in the south of France. In fact, we don't try to obtain any, since it can be taken from cuttings. Potato tubers are used in the same way as potato tubers; there's nothing more delicate about them than the good varieties of the latter plant, so in Paris, potatoes are only grown as an object of fantasy and curiosity. The same is true of the Chinese yam, which is easier to cultivate and, for this reason, will probably compete seriously with Parmentier's tubers in a few years' time. AGRICULTURAL GROWING High rents, limited space in Paris and its suburbs, rising labor costs and all kinds of overheads have forced market gardeners to banish vegetable plants from their gardens, as they take up too much soil, require too much space and do not require constant care. On the other hand, the same causes produced the same effects: farmers in the greater suburbs were forced to give up growing cereals, as they could not compete with distant producers whose rent and labor costs remained relatively low. As a result, they gave themselves over on a large scale to the cultivation of vegetable plants neglected by market gardeners, so that the outskirts of Paris, for several leagues around, present, almost without interruption, the appearance of an immense vegetable garden. We won't go back over the plants we've already discussed in depth in the section on market gardening; this chapter will only deal with those plants for which we've reserved it, as indicated by the cross-references in chapter v. Artichokes, asparagus and beet are grown by suburban growers using the methods we have described, so we won't return to them here. Carrots. Growers only produce large, long red and long yellow carrots for market in winter. They are sown at the same time as market garden carrots for winter consumption, and require the same care. These carrots can be recognized at the market by their short heads and the tie that runs through them. Others are sold as Crécy carrots in bunches with their tops. Cabbage. Quintal cabbage. - Requires deep plowing and abundant manuring. It is sown in the first half of March, a little light, in nurseries, thinned out as needed and planted in April, very light, as this type of cabbage produces enormous apples. It's the most prized cabbage for making sauerkraut. Harvesting takes place in October and November. Early Savoy cabbage. - It is sown in May, planted in June and July, and starts to grow in late autumn; it is kept until March. Ordinary Savoy cabbage. - Sown in February and March, planted in April, harvested in June, July and August. Savoy cabbage, known as Vertus. - Sow from April to June, 60 cm apart. They need to be edged and watered. When winter arrives, we pull up all the cabbages and put them in a trench with their heads facing north, avoiding the sun, which is destructive after the frost. However, if the frost is very severe, cover the plants with manure or straw mats. Another method is to place the cabbages, root up, in a plough furrow, which is then covered by tracing a parallel furrow. However, the soil must be covered in frosty weather. Brussels sprouts. - Sow from February to June. They are planted in areas where early potatoes have been harvested, and the small apples that sprout from the leaf axils are harvested from October through the winter. Non-headed and large-ribbed cabbage - Sowing takes place in June, planting in July and August, and harvesting of leaves 18. takes place throughout the winter until May.

Kohlrabi and turnip cabbage. - They are sown in May and June, and planted in July, with 35 to 40 centimetres between plants. Like all cabbages, they need water, deep ploughing and good manuring. As soon as frost approaches, these root cabbages should be placed in a greenhouse. Crambé, or sea cabbage. - Sow in March, or transplant. It can be planted with cuttings, which speeds up harvesting. Cuttings are taken in pots under a frame. For this purpose, the roots are cut to 6 or 8 centimetres in length and planted again in pots on a warm layer. They are treated like any other plant reproduced in this way, and then planted out. We've already talked about forced cultivation. When we leave it in the open air, we have to butter it. We start this operation at the end of January and continue it every fortnight, in order to have several successive harvests. To butter each plant, pour a 16-centimeter-thick pile of potting soil or light earth over it, and cover it with a good bed of manure or leaves to stimulate vegetation. A month later, when the leaves begin to appear, we cut them off at ground level, taking care to spare the eyes below. The plant is ridged again, and a second harvest is made in the same way, after which the ridges are removed. This plant can then be forced like asparagus, as mentioned above. Shallot. The shallot is a lily native to Palestine. It is propagated by planting bulbs in February or March, 6 to 8 centimetres apart and almost flush with the ground. Choose the thinnest and longest bulbs for planting. This plant does not like water. It requires some hoeing in summer. The largest bulbs are sold. Those reserved for planting should be put in a dry place after being exposed to the sun for two or three days. Shallots are used for seasoning. Broad beans. Julienne, marsh, Windsor and green beans are grown in the open ground. Sowing takes place in February, March and April in well-tilled soil that has been smoked or amended by a previous crop. Sowing is done in clumps or in rays; hoeing and pinching are used, as described for the early cultivation of bog beans. Beans. Field seedlings are sown in April and May at the bottom of hills and in good exposure. Siliceous soils are preferred for this crop. Laon hâtif, flageolet, soissons nain, Massy, Suisse and Bagnolet varieties are sown. Once the soil is well loosened, amended and sufficiently dry, sow in clumps using a spade, rake or hoe. Two people can do the sowing quickly. One makes the hole, the other puts in the seed, which the second covers with the soil removed for the second hole. Five or six beans are placed in each hole, spaced 25 to 30 centimetres apart in all directions. Careful growers take a little longer and cover each hole with a small shovelful of compost. Beans are also sown in rays 4 to 5 centimetres deep, in twos or threes 12 to 15 centimetres apart. Cover with 2 or 3 centimetres of soil or compost. As soon as they have developed, the rays are folded back and hoeing is carried out as needed. All these beans are harvested green. If the soil is damp, don't sow before May 15. Beans to be harvested dry are sown in May and early June. The varieties used are the Soissons bean, the sabre bean, the Prague bean, the butter bean and the predomme. They are sown in clumps 25 to 30 centimeters apart, and raked before they are rowed. Rakes are small poles, sharpened at one end to rest on the ground, and one is spiked between two clumps. Beans climb after the oars, which are brought in after harvest for use in subsequent years. You can use twigs from faggots for the reams, but harvesting is not as easy and you gain nothing, apart from the fact that it's not as pleasant to look at. As all the beans are not dried at the same time, they have to be picked one after the other, and the pods have to be dried in the shade. When the crop occupies a very large area, we don't take so many precautions: we pull up the plant with its seeds, dry it in clumps by suspending it by the foot in the air or in attics, and in many localities we beat it with a flail. It's a good idea to hull only those that you reserve for personal use, as they cook more easily and taste better... Turnip. This native cruciferous plant is broadcast from March 15th to September 1st, at a rate of 30 grams per acre. Turnips for winter consumption should be sown in the first half of August. When they are strong enough, they are thinned out. Seedlings sown during the summer should be watered; otherwise they set seed without taproot. Turnips start selling in June and continue until April. We pull them up before the frosts and pile them in a pit 1 meter wide and 80 centimeters deep, after cutting off the head. We cover the pit with straw to protect it from the frosts. The following varieties are grown: Long des Vertus, Croissy turnip, Rose du Palatinat, Freneuse, Morigny and Meaux gray turnips, early flat white, early flat red, Scottish yellow, Stone spherical yellow, Golden ball turnip and Astringham turnip. Onions. We have dealt elsewhere with the cultivation of white onions. Red and yellow onions are grown as agricultural crops. Field onions are sown in the first half of March at a rate of 100 grams of seed per acre. The seedbed is harrowed with a fork. The soil must have been well ploughed and smoked, or only amended with previous crops. It would be a good idea, which is almost never done, to cover the seed with a good layer of compost. The expense would certainly be amply covered by the product of the seed, which the wind would not scatter. It's a mistake to smoke when sowing; the land set aside for this crop should be smoked after harvest. Onions should be weeded frequently, and thinned out if they are too tightly packed. If water is readily available, it's a good idea to water in dry weather, and as soon as they have reached maturity, you can speed them up by passing a roller or an empty barrel over the tops. Onions are harvested as much as possible in dry weather, and left to mature on the ground. They can be stored in a well-ventilated attic, laid out on the ground or suspended in bunches from the ceiling, after the tops have been woven. Pale red onions are sown very thickly in May or June, to produce small onions that can be harvested in October or November. Onions stored in the attic are transplanted in February, 10 or 11 centimetres apart in all directions. They are harvested in July and August. Growers also sow large white onions in February and March, treating them in the same way as red onions. Red onions are harvested from seed in exactly the same way as white onions. Preserved onions must be protected from frost, although they are not very sensitive to it. They should be covered if it freezes hard, or placed in a room with a fire. They should also be protected from humidity, which causes them to sprout. Dandelion. Dandelion grows spontaneously in meadows, and is sought after as a healthy, refreshing salad at a time when others are largely lacking. Growers around Paris sow dandelions in spring in ploughed and amended soil. They transplant them in June and July and cover them with soil or compost in autumn. The leaves are then white and more attractive than those of wild chicory. If you're not transplanting, it's a good idea to cover spring seedlings in the autumn with good soil or even potting compost, for a better harvest. The winged seed of the dandelion flies away at the slightest breeze, so harvest it in the morning when the dew falls, and dry it. It lasts three years. Eultivated corn. A legume native to southern Europe. Since the railroads have brought produce from the south to Paris in a matter of hours, market gardeners have abandoned the early cultivation of peas, which they used to do in the following way, which is still well known to those with kitchen gardens. Sow in November in two or three cold frames. Once the plant has emerged, prepare as many frames as you want to keep. Place them with their boxes in a well-exposed spot; in a "costière" for example, make shelves and spade the peas with their clods. As the vegetation develops, the peas are laid down, as is done for early beans, and they soon flower, so they are pinched off above the fourth flower. Be careful not to throw away the pinched ends, as they make excellent soup. Ventilate as needed, water if the weather is too dry, and harvest as soon as the seed has formed. Sowing can continue under a frame or on a warm bed in February for replanting in the open air. These plantings should be covered with mats or litter in frosty weather. Pinching and watering are essential. Species grown in this way are : Prince Albert, the early dwarf, the ordinary Michaux pea and the Michaux de Hollande, which is the earliest. Growers sow the Michaux de Hollande, Michaux de Rueil and petits pois de Paris varieties in November and December at midday exposure. They choose siliceous soils and sow two liters per acre. These seedlings are sown in rays and covered with potting soil, which can be bought from market gardeners at this time of year. Once the seedlings have sprouted, the rays are filled in by hoeing; ripening is activated by pinching. The last sowings, in February and March, are of Clamart peas and wrinkled peas. In July, Auvergne peas and peas without parchment are sown. The land occupied by the first peas is then used to grow carrots, turnips, potatoes, savoy cabbage and so on. The plants to be used as seedbeds are sown in the field, pulled out before they are completely dried out, placed in heaps and, once they have begun to ferment, dried out. The seed lasts five years. The first field potato season takes place in February and March in good exposure and includes the following varieties: marjoram, Schaw, early dwarf, Abstone Quidney, etc. Planting is done with a spade, using healthy, medium-sized tubers. Plant in a checkerboard pattern, 30 to 35 centimetres apart in all directions, place a little soil on top and fill in with potting soil or well-made slurry. Cultivate and hoe as needed. This first season's produce is harvested before full maturity. These potatoes are replaced by Michaux peas for sale in September, or by a planting of savoy cabbage. Only those plants reserved for seed are left to ripen. The second season is planted a little lighter in March and April and receives the same care. They are also harvested before maturity. These potatoes are for winter consumption and for sowing. Those destined for the latter must be weeded and hoeed, and the land must have been ploughed several times and well fertilized with manure. Only those potatoes that have been harvested from seed should be harvested. The berry is left to ripen on the stalk, then, after picking, it is crushed in water to separate it from the pulp and left to dry in the shade. The seed lasts for three years. Tubers for planting must be harvested when ripe. They are left on the ground and in the air, where they turn green; they are then brought in dry and placed in crates without being pressed. They must be protected from humidity and frost, but not deprived of air, otherwise they would grow. These are the care requirements for early varieties. Winter potatoes are harvested in September and October. When they are fully ripe and the tops have dried out. If the weather is dry, it's a good idea to leave the tubers in the air for a while. As soon as they are dry, we pile them up and lock them in airless cellars, where they are shuffled every ten or twelve days when frost threatens. When frosts are no longer to be feared, it's a good idea to take them out of the cellars, where they would grow, and put them under cover in a dry, well-ventilated place such as an attic. Horseradish. Horseradish is a cruciferous plant that can be propagated by planting sections of its root in autumn in well-ploughed, well-smoked soil. This plant requires frequent hoeing and weeding, and is only harvested after three years. It's the roots that are in demand. Grated, they are used as mustard and are known as German mustard or capuchin mustard. This plant is not allowed to go to seed; it would take too long to grow from seed. Salsify and scorzonera. These plants are native composites that grow spontaneously in meadows and whose succulent root, sought-after for food, has led to the introduction of cultivation in the Paris area. Sowing takes place in March, April and May, either broadcast or in rows, and requires 120 to 130 grams of seed per acre. The soil must be well fertilized and deeply ploughed to accommodate the taproot of these plants; however, it is preferable for the fertilizer to come from a previous harvest. These plants would benefit from a good basinage if water were available nearby at the time of emergence. If the seedbed is too thick, it should be thinned out. Sales take place from October to spring. In winter, pull out all the roots, put them in gauges and protect them from frost with a layer of dry manure. To harvest from seed, the best roots are selected in autumn and replanted; in spring, the salsify flowers and the seed is harvested in July, in the morning with the dew, because its wings blow away in the slightest wind. The seed lasts for two years. Scorzonera differs from salsify in its black bark, and is sown less thickly, needing only 100 to 110 grams of seed per acre. It flowers as early as the first year, so seeds can be obtained at this time; however, it is generally treated in the same way as salsify. Engrals for agricultural crops. We have seen that market gardeners use only horse manure, and cow manure when they cannot obtain sufficient quantities of the former. Farmers use all types of manure, and in the Paris area, they mainly use the fertilizer known as slurry. Sludge is the product of street sweeping. This product, used in Paris for two centuries by contractors, is the most widespread fertilizer for crops in the greater suburbs. What is swept up each day is collected in piles and left to ferment. This consists of plant and animal products, in the center of which considerable heat soon develops, while an enormous mass of hydrogen sulfide is released from the detritus. To preserve its value, the sludge must be prevented from losing this gas, which is why it is handled with a pitchfork when putrid fermentation becomes too much of a problem. It is even sometimes necessary to stop it, especially in hot, humid weather; this is achieved by adding plaster to the mass. We call green sludge that which has been recently collected, and made sludge that which has been fermenting for six months to a year. The average price of sludge is 9 francs per cubic meter, delivered 15 kilometers from Paris. Its useful effect is said to last four years. It is mainly to turnip, cabbage and other cruciferous crops that slurry manure is applied, with good reason, as this sulfurous fertilizer is perfectly suited to plants that owe their properties to the presence of sulfur in their tissues. These properties of sludge had given rise to hopes that detritus deposited in sewers, and even sewage containing suspended organic matter, could be put to good use in cultivation. We will give a brief history of the experiments carried out to this end, as we intend to discuss them in greater depth in a special monograph. Sewage and sewage deposits. A few years ago, it was reported in France that, in response to a complaint from the residents of the Thames, an attempt had been made in England to use sewage water to rid the river of the foul, foul stench whose emanations had disturbed the very sittings of Parliament. It was said that sewage was a rich and powerful fertilizer that had been neglected for too long, and that was left to the tides, which distributed it charitably to both banks of the Thames, where it maintained the green meadows. Immediately, the Parisian city council conceived the idea of ridding the people living along the Seine of the emanations of the collector sewer, by using the waters of this vast cesspool in the same way as the people living along the Thames use the waters of London. An experimental field was set up in Clichy, and two vast reservoirs were dug in the middle of the field to receive some of the sewer water. This water, treated in the same way as sewage, is received in the two basins, where it deposits the solids it holds in suspension. The experimental field covers a 14. vast area where market garden plants are grown in the open ground, with no fertilizer other than Paris sewage water and the deposits it leaves behind. The water is distributed to the plants by means of gutters running between the rows of plantations; this is more or less the method used by the Amiens hortillons. Deposits are used as manure and buried. In our opinion, two years of experimentation have not yet produced conclusive results, the land occupied by the field having been previously smoked and covered in the first year with a thick layer of fertilizing silt by two successive floods of the Seine. The water and deposits seemed to contain a certain number of nitrogenous products, but the alumina and iron salts, either from the soil or from the materials used for disinfection, by opposing the decomposition of these products, are, in our opinion, detrimental to this fertilizer, to which we attribute an intermediate value between the pitch and the pond silt, i.e. the value of a good soil improver. We hope that longer experience will disprove our initial impressions, and it may well be that it will, once the Bièvre has poured the quota of its highly nitrogenous products into the sewer. If we consider the two methods of removing products useful to agriculture, in France and England, it will be easy to see the different results obtained in these two countries. In London, no emptying is carried out; all products are carried away by the water in the cabinets, aptly named water-closets, and thrown into the nearest sewer. Hence the high nitrogen content of London's sewage. In Paris, none of this happens. The sewers used to receive all the black water from cesspools, but this is now the exception. The quantity of nitrogen can therefore only result from the urine, diluted in a large quantity of water, of men and horses, and the sewage, cleaned by devices, of the silica it contains and the organic debris that floats at its snrface, In addition to a few insoluble parts in suspension, the water now contains only a dissolution of a triple salt, a kind of ammoniacal alum containing iron, in short, a triple sulfate of alumina, ammonia and iron. The deposit has no odour, or a simple mouldy smell, as the aforementioned salts prevent fermentation; however, the larvae feast on it, as we have seen, proving that there are fermentable parts, but they are in an environment where the plant roots don't like to go looking for them. When watering with a hose or ordinary watering can, the water itself retains enough soapy or aluminous matter in suspension to obstruct the pores of the green parts of the plants and harm their vegetation. The pages we have just read were already written, when we learned that, on the basis of a favorable report from a committee of the Horticultural Society, the municipal administration of Paris intended to carry out cultivation trials on a larger scale on the Gennevilliers plain, using fertilizer from its sewage system. We would therefore like to give more detailed information on the trials carried out in Clichy, and to publish the results of our personal and unofficial observations. As we suspected, the separating fiquid is alumina sulfate. The deposit is therefore indeed the sulfate we mentioned, and the traces of iron we found there can be sufficiently explained by the wear and tear of horse shoes on the Paris pavement. We have often visited the Clichy experimental field, and, it must be said, not in a spirit of disparagement, but with a desire to learn from it. Our observations cover three years. The soil of this now-destroyed field comprised: 1° a market-gardening part, following the methods of the Amiens hortillists; 2° an agricultural part, including cereals and field crops; 3° a fence planted with espaliered fruit trees. We don't have to count the first year of installation, but by the second year the land was being fully cultivated according to the procedures adopted, and the results were beginning to be recorded. Whenever we visited the field during this year (1867), we were struck by the difference in the produce compared with their similar market gardens. Vegetables were half as developed as their marsh-grown counterparts. The same was true of agricultural produce, especially beet and corn. It was a wet year, and experience dictated that the fertilizer had to be applied wet, so we did not comment on the value of the sewage water. In 1868, Clichy's cultivation engineers were favored by an exceptional summer, and were able to rival the best-understood market gardeners for pumpkin and cardoon. What's so surprising? These are plants that like plenty of sun for their stems and water for their roots. Salads, despite an apparent state of prosperity in their youth, have almost all melted by harvest time. Cabbage and apples produced only very ordinary produce. Leeks and carrots were very mediocre. Tomatoes produced little, although the fruit was quite beautiful, but ripened very late. Three-quarters of the melons planted were destroyed, the remaining quarter surviving only because of the year's exceptional heat. White celery performed fairly well, while celeriac was less successful. We noted a fine and good crop of corn as an agricultural crop, and the year was exceptional for this crop; but beet was inferior to that of the plain. Potatoes were largely lost, as was the case everywhere. The bizarre and poorly formed tubers, and the numerous welds, prove that the cultivation method used is unfavorable to them. Here is the most serious of our observations: On a 4-metre surface of beans, we counted twenty clumps; a market gardener would have put in forty to forty-five clumps. On the same surface area of celeriac: eighteen plants; in a market garden there would have been forty-eight plants, which would have been double in size. In white celery, we counted twenty plants on the 4-metre surface, where we put fifty-six plants, certainly more beautiful. For the same measure of land, we counted twenty-four feet of escarole, we put forty-eight; eight feet of apple cabbage, we put thirty-six; and so on. That's where the whole crop comes in, with the gutters taking up half the land. There is no possibility of establishing seedlings and counterplants in crops grown in this way, and despite this, the produce obtained is very mediocre. Only fifteen or twenty leagues from Paris could sewage water compete with Amiens hortillons. As for the market gardeners of Paris, these experiments have taught them nothing, except that they are equal to their mission, and that this over-praised fertilizer is not the way forward. The agricultural section of the experimental garden gave us no better results. We counted eighteen plants of maize on a 9-metre surface, where ordinary cultivation would put in twice as many. We counted twenty-one very mediocre beet plants on 9 meters of surface area, a space occupied by at least sixty-three beet plants and at most eighty-one on the plain, with better quality roots. It was all there. But could the 48 hectares of land rented for 15 or 1,800 francs per hectare, which would have equalled those of the market gardeners, get by if such a considerable amount of space were required for the plants grown? Obviously not, since engineers have not been more successful in an exceptional terrain and in an exceptional year. So market gardeners will probably be sticking to their good horse manure for a long time to come. We've been told that sewage is a first-rate fertilizer. We took a certain quantity of the material made available to us, and dried it by spreading it out in thin layers; it lost 20 p. 0/0 of its weight and gave off no odor during this drying process. One kilogram of this desiccated material calcined in a roasting kiln lost 200 grams of organic matter, while potting soil lost 800 grams under the same conditions. Leaching followed by further desiccation resulted in a loss of only around 50 grams of soluble matter, while the potting soil residue lost a further 100 grams. Between this so-called fertilizer and potting soil, which for us is little more than an amen dement, decide and choose; as for us, our choice is made. THE MARKET GARDEN YEAR. We think it would be useful to set out here, in the form of a memento, the work to be carried out each month on a market-gardening farm. We have accompanied our prescriptions with plates showing the author's garden and the crops it contains at each monthly period. Under the heading of particular observations, we give the changes that can be made to crops at the same time by preferring other plants to those we have adopted. The market-gardening year begins in October. By this time, almost all the summer crops have disappeared, and we start preparing the crops that will produce a harvest in the spring. Work in October. All cold and hot cultivation equipment must be in working order. We start unpacking the bells, setting aside the small wooden cleats we've placed at the top of each one. The bells are prepared, covered with 1 or 2 centimetres of potting soil spread with a rake, knocked into the planking and three rows of bells per bells are placed, 2 or 3 centimetres apart. The compost is broken and formed into banks, with any excess removed. Mushroom growers prepare their wheels. Sowing - Lamb's lettuce and regency in white onion beds and in beds reserved for next spring's melons. - Spinach in nurseries, to be replanted between warm lettuces. - Chervil broadcast among escarole and chicory. - Cover wild chicory with a frame. - Chives, pink and red radishes, in teens until the 9th, then under cloches. - Black and green romaine lettuces until the 10th - Gotte and Georges lettuces, blond romaines, red and gray lettuces until the end of the month. - White onions and leeks to remain in place until the end of the month. Planting. - Transplanting in cloches, 1° by twenty-four black lettuces for stretcher beds, 2° by twelve green romaines when hot, 3° by twenty-four of the same when cold, 4° by twenty-four or thirty blond romaines, Georges, Gotte, gray and red lettuces. -Transplanting cauliflowers under frame by two or three hundred. - Planting white onions in the previous month's lamb's lettuce seedlings. - Planting of black lettuces under cloches for cold work, four or five per cloche and forty to fifty per frame. Care for growing crops. - If the weather is dry, continue to water celery and cauliflower, and cabbage and onion plantations. If the sun is too strong afterwards, cover with straw mats for one or two days. Don't put in potting soil or cloches until everything is ready for transplanting, otherwise the sun will dry out the potting soil. White celery is covered with mulch or bedding to blanch it, escarole, chicory and romaine are tied, cardoons are stuffed, white celery is started to deepen and given good wetting, and weeding is carried out thoroughly. Harvesting. - Last artichokes, red beet, spinach, cardoons, celeriac and white celery, carrots, chervil, mushrooms, curly endive, cauliflower, broccoli, apple cabbage, savoy cabbage, Brussels sprouts, pumpkin, cress, watercress, tarragon, lettuces, lamb's lettuce, sorrel, turnips, parsnips, parsley, peppers, pimiento, leeks, pear, pink, red and black radishes, purslane, saisifis, scorzonera, tomatoes, green asparagus (early). Special observations. - Instead of the crops shown on this month's plan, another market gardener could have grown the following, which might have been more advantageous to him from the point of view of his clientele, as each one directs his work so as to meet the needs of those who buy from him, so that the halls are always supplied with all vegetable plants. We could have replaced celery on soil with cauliflower, escarole with celeriac, cauliflower with cardoons, white celery on beds with escarole, spring onions with spinach, celeriac with carrots, endives with curly endive, outgoing cauliflower with endives or white celery, civet with lamb's lettuce, white onions with escarole, chervil and lamb's lettuce with white celery. 8° ;. . des V 44 K R.HII "===-- LR pi e A S CTI FN * be "---: l. Work in November. Potting soil is broken and formed into chains from which the extra is removed. Manure is carried. The first layers of black lettuce are turned over in January, followed by carrots and lettuce. We smoke the plots reserved for oxheart cabbage, set up the mushroom wheels, warm up the asparagus, sorrel and tarragon, start taking out the frames and trunks and wash the bells. Sowing. - On beds: early round short carrots, combined with a few spinach seedlings or transplants. - Spinach or small lettuces on cabbage racks and in empty lots for May melons. - Cauliflower Sainte-Thérèse under cloches. - Peas under frames. Plantations. - Black lettuce and spinach, sorrel roots to warm up. - On shelves: beef heart cabbage. 15. - Along walls: Passion lettuces. Care for growing crops. - Butter artichokes, cellar beet, recess white celery before the 10th and pour the soil into the recess before the 20th for fear of frost; frozen celery is lost if touched; uproot celeriac and gauge it under 10 centimetres of soil; give air to the romaine and lettuce cloches, using forks made from barrel staves with three notches cut in them; push the romaine plants back in at the end of the month, put back in at nineteen for those at twenty-four, put back in at six or eight for those at twelve, weed, cover the plants with straw mats if the temperature drops to 3 or 4 degrees below zero. Monthly harvests. - Warmed white asparagus, layered green asparagus, layered sorrel, cardoons, carrots, white celery, celeriac, chervil, mushrooms, curly endive, wild chicory, cauliflower, savoy cabbage, Brussels sprouts, Brussels sprouts, spring onions, pumpkins, watercress, black lettuces, lamb's lettuce, turnips, parsley, leeks, periwinkle, pink, red and black radishes, raiponce, salsify, scorzonera. Special remarks. - Layers do not change place. We can replace (see monthly plan) escarole by white celery, white celery by apple cabbage, spinach by lamb's lettuce in teens, lamb's lettuce by chicory bays, white celery by celeriac, apple cabbage with sorrel, parsley or spring onion, the latter with civet, white onions with chervil, the latter with spinach, civet with lamb's lettuce, apple cabbage with chervil. III Work in December. We continue to plow and smoke the apple-cabbage fields. Layers are actively made. Potting soil banks and manure chains are prepared, and the soil is covered to protect it from frost and keep it in good condition for working in due course. We take out the frames and bells, and prepare the asparagus, sorrel and tarragon stoves. Cloche layers for romaine and mushroom millstones are made. The necessary plowing for cold work is done. Sowing. - In layers: carrots and spinach, pink and red radishes. Under cold frames: parsley, sorrel, celery, parsnips, carrots. Layered at the end of the month: wild chicory, peas. Planting. - Plant thirty to thirty-six black lettuces under each frame with a spinach transplant, one green or blond romaine per cloche on layers, press green and blond romaines under cloches, pull out cauliflower plants under frames and press them in, transplant Sainte-Thérèse cauliflowers under cloches or under frames, cold-plant twenty green romaines, thirty Georges lettuces, thirty Gotte lettuces under frames, plant apple cabbages. Care for growing crops. - Butter cardoons, cover them in times of frost, cover artichoke buttages with dry manure, rework asparagus paths and warm them up, make good accots around the layers, fill paths as needed with dry manure, peel black lettuces under frames, cover these frames with mats every evening. Stuff cardoons, take them down to the cellar if possible, bury carrots and cover them with dry manure, warm up mushroom wheels, cover beds with straw mats if the temperature drops to 4 or 5 degrees below zero, put cauliflowers in gauges under frames or take them down to the cellar, put pumpkins and giraumons in greenhouses, cover watercress beds with water, protect turnips, kohlrabi, onions and parsnips from frost, mulch parsley and sorrel, cover potatoes with a large layer of bedding, protect salsify, scorzonera, broccoli and savoy cabbage from frost, turn down frames and cloches in times of frost, reinforce apple-cabbage plants on teens. Harvest of the month. - White and green asparagus, cardoons, carrots, celery and celeriac, chervil, mushrooms, black lettuce, lamb's lettuce, sorrel, parsley, pear, pink, red and black radishes, raiponce, salsify, scorzonera. Specific comments. - Plan transformation. - Layers occupy the same position. - Cold lettuces can be replaced by white celery, white celery by lamb's lettuce and white onion, apple cabbage by spring onions and lamb's lettuce, teenagers by cold frames, celeriac by teenagers, white onion by apple cabbage or spinach, lamb's lettuce and chervil, chervil by lamb's lettuce. Work in January. All frames and bells are in active service, repairs are made at S ~ 4 6 2 6. A CUCHYLAGARENNE 4 LPONCE Moitieusteur Sarateb¬ꝯ Graeaux du Jlloio de Jauvier. la -Rerolte. P. 267. 4 20 a à faire les couches à châssis, à cloches et à air. We start ploughing the costières. Care must be taken to break up the clods, smoke well and never plow in rainy weather. Sowing. - Short carrots, radishes, leeks, first melons, first tomatoes, turnips. - Under cold frames and cloches: carrots, radishes; they can also be sown in layers in the air. - In costières: carrots, radishes, peas. - On hot beds: spinach and curly endive. - Under cold cloches or on small frames: second-season romaine, Bonneuil cabbage, cauliflower; in open ground, spring onions. Planting. - In the ground: apple and beef cabbage. - On stretcher beds: black lettuces. - Under cloches: three lettuces and one romaine - Under cold cloches: three romaines or four Georges or Gotte lettuces, three red or gray lettuces - In the costiere at the end of the month: romaines and lettuces. Care for growing crops. - In frosty weather, same care as in December. When the thaw arrives, uncover the plants, remove the manure, provide air under the frames on the opposite side to the wind; uncover the celery, otherwise it will rot, reshape the paths, peel the lettuces, pull out the weeds, dry the mats, care for the mushroom wheels, put on new shirts and warm up the paths if necessary. Harvesting. - Asparagus (white and green), cardoons, carrots (callets), celery (white and celeriac), chervil, mushrooms, chicory (wild), kale, kohlrabi, spring onions, watercress, spinach, tarragon, black lettuce, lamb's lettuce, turnips, sorrel, parsnips, parsley, leeks, pumpkins (yellow and green), radishes (red and pink), radishes (black), raiponce, salsify, scorzonera. Special notes. - The lettuce frames to be turned over will become air radishes or cloche layers. The other layers will be at the same points. Squares of lettuce and cold romaine can be replaced by ados, celery and spinach by mâches, ados by lettuce or cold romaine, celery by ray spinach and baby lettuce, apple cabbage by white onion, civet by spring onion, and vice versa, onion by apple cabbage and mâches by onion. The costières could be planted with romaine and lettuce. Work in February. The same care is given to the frames and bells as in the previous month; all the glass is outside. Air is supplied to the sashes on the opposite side to the wind, and to the bells, always on the south side. All sash layers must be finished; only bell or air layers remain. All the previous year's melon trenches are emptied, and used to top the beds. All the land is ploughed and smoked. Rake and spread potting soil over the potting soils. Sowing. - Carrots, pink and red radishes are sown in cloaca and air layers. - Carrots and radishes are sown on costières and counter-planks. - Under cold frames and cloches: sorrel, parsley, chervil, curly endive, wild chicory, cauliflower, Bonneuil cabbage, savoy cabbage, red cabbage, Brussels sprouts, eggplant, asparagus, borage, bulbous chervil, celery. - On hot layers: curly endive, celery, broad beans, watercress, beans, lettuce, romaine, melons, potatoes, pepper, purslane, tomatoes. - On soil: red onions, peas. Planting and transplanting. - Black and green romaine lettuces are planted on trays or cloches; Georges and Gotte lettuces on air beds, green romaine and Georges lettuces on cold trays and cloches, gray and red lettuces in pots or on counter-plates. Flageolet beans are planted on hot beds, and melons sown the previous month are transplanted. Transplant nursery seedlings from the previous month, such as apple cabbage and white onions; plant garlic, shallots and civets. Care for growing crops. - Asparagus and mushroom stoves continue. We fill in the paths, reshape the beds, wet the layer manures if they are too dry, peel the lettuces and romaine, weed, ventilate the frames during the day and cover them at night. Harvesting. - This is the start of harvesting the layers that provide abundant lettuces, black and red radishes, spinach, green romaine, white and green asparagus. Cardoons, callet carrots, celery and celeriac, chervil, mushrooms, wild chicory, savoy cabbage, spring onions, green pumpkins, watercress, spinach and tarragon, lamb's lettuce, turnips, sorrel, parsnips, parsley, dandelions, leeks, pink, red and black radishes, raiponce, salsify and scorzonera are, as in January, the main crops of the month. Special observations. Air beds can be replaced by bell beds, and vice versa; cold frames can be replaced by a square of raipons, celery by apple cabbage, spinach by onions, teens by a square prepared for the first melons, white celery and celeriac by flageolet beans, white celery and celeriac with flageolet beans, apple cabbage with white celery, chives with melon and tomato plants, white celery with chives, white onions with spinach seedlings and baby lettuces. Early apple cabbage could be planted in the costières, and white onions in the counter-planting. VI Work in March. The frames for melons, beans and tomatoes are moved to their rightful place.
