N°53 A CUCNYLAGARENNE 4 LPONCE RPœ n Lai Mortieulteur Maraicba Gracaux Ou licio d Jllats. Rérolte. P.278. 8 51 bes a- land for ados, celery, melons, tomatoes, cucumbers, beans. The manure is used to make the beds for these plants. If the manure is dry, it is used to make well-manicured beds, which are wetted for a few days before use. If not, chains are made immediately, and the beds continue to be earthworked and the old trenches emptied. Sowing. - March is sowing month. Each part of the garden gets its own seedbed. Pink and red radishes, carrots, leeks, spring onions, spinach, asparagus and arrowroot are sown in full squares and other seedbeds. In warm conditions, sow eggplants, basil, borage, baselle, white celery, Rouen chicory, flageolet beans, melons, chillies and potatoes. - Sow beans in cold frames. Old bed: beans, white mustard. In open ground: strawberries, fennel, chervil, bulbous chervil, savoy, quintal, Bonneuil, Brussels sprouts, cauliflower, broccoli, crambe cabbage, turnips, red onions, sorrel, salad burnet, dandelions, leeks, peas, purslane, salsify, scorzonera, savory, tetragona, tomato, thyme. Artichokes are propagated by suckers, and oxalis by cuttings. Planting, transplanting and disadvantages. - All plants sown during the previous month are transplanted by cuttings: melons, cucumbers, eggplants and others. Blonde romaines, grey and red lettuces are planted in full squares; Rouen chicory, melons, cucumbers and beans are bedded. Cauliflower is counter-planted on layers, cold bays and soil. We plant garlic, shallots and leeks on layers, civets, asparagus, tarragon, potatoes in good shelter; we multiply Chinese yams by their bulbils. Care for growing crops. - If the season continues to be rigorous, we give the same care to beds and forced crops as in previous months. Air beds should be fitted with mats. When the sun is too hot, cover all new plantings for a few days from 11 a.m. to 2 a.m.. The plantations are given a few drops of water. Watering starts at the end of the month if the weather is hot and dry, and is carried out under cover when necessary. Harvesting. - See previous month, and add purslane and potatoes to the new Special Observations. - The air layers of the plan would become cucumber layers, those with frames cloche layers and vice versa. Lettuces would be replaced by melons, tomatoes by beans, melons would be replaced by onions, apple cabbages, lamb's lettuce, raipons, civets and apple cabbages by preparations for melons; spring onions by blond romaines and cauliflowers; white onions by sedges or romaines, cauliflowers, radishes and spinach. All costières would be planted with green romaine or lettuce. VII Work in April All the equipment has to change places, with frames and cloches reserved for melons, cucumbers, beans and tomatoes. Work on the beds and trenches is in full swing, with ploughing and manuring completed. Mushroom millstones begin to be shredded for mulch. Seedlings. - Arrochokes, eggplants and basil on old layers, nasturtiums at the foot of the walls, borage, baselle, carrots, white celery, cardoons on old layers and on soil at the 25th, chervil, wild chicory, curly endive, Milan cabbage, Vertus cabbage, broccoli, spring onions, yellow cucumbers, pumpkins on old layers, watercress and perennials on soil, spinach in other seflageolets on soil in good exposure. Layered melons, white mustard, turnips, parsnips, parsley, layered chillies, salad burnet, leeks, leeks, peas, potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes; in other seeds, salsify, scorzonera, savory, layered tomatoes, old corn. Planting, transplanting and disadvantages. - All the previous month's seedlings are transplanted into the nursery. Asparagus and eggplant are planted in hot cloches. We start planting cold chicory in trays and cloches. Bonneuil cabbage, cauliflower and broccoli are counter-planted with romaine and lettuce. We plant ordinary savoy cabbages, quintal cabbages, Brussels sprouts, cucumbers on layers, cold flageolet beans under frames and cloches, romaine, lettuces, melons on layers or trenches, leeks, tomatoes on trenches under frames and cloches as well as on soil under frames and cloches. Thyme borders, tarragon 16. clumps and Chinese yam bulblets can be propagated. Care for growing crops. - Care should be taken to weed, ventilate the frames and cloches, and cover them with mulch or light mulch when the sun is hot. All plantings under glass require constant care during this month. Plants are wetted immediately after planting. If the weather is dry, start watering carrot beds, cauliflowers, onions and apple cabbages. Salads should be watered moderately, mainly in the morning if the weather is dry and warm. If it's cold, they'll rust. Radishes need plenty of water. Layers and trenches for melons should be made of well-wetted manure. Start pruning and topping young melon and cucumber plants. Harvesting. - Early crops are beginning to produce. We finish harvesting white and green asparagus, and start cutting open-ground asparagus in good exposure. The same is true of artichokes under chassis and those with good exposure. We harvest bedding carrots, new chervil, early apple cabbage, new cauliflower at the end of the month, green cucumbers, spring onions, civets, watercress, spinach, tarragon, broad beans, forced strawberries, beans, lettuces, romaine, lamb's lettuce, regency, white mustard, turnips, white onions towards the end of the month, sorrel, parsnips, parsley, leeks, peas, new potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes, raiponce, salsify, scorzonera, sea cabbage. Special remarks. - Tomatoes can be replaced by carrots, cauliflower by carrots, and cloche layers by tomatoes, which would also replace romaine and cold lettuces. Tomatoes would be replaced by melons, the latter by cold-grown chicory and beans. Cabbages would be replaced by cucumbers, chives by chervil, and vice versa. Apple cabbages would take the place of white onions, red lettuces that of romaine; blondes, and the costières would be occupied by early apple cabbages or onions. VIII Work in May. All the glass is out and used. There must not be a single unoccupied spot in the field. Frame and bell trenches are completed. The sash layers are turned over, a job to be done during the month. The horse harnesses are repaired or replaced, the carriage repaired or repainted, or a new one purchased. Doormats are brought into the shed, repaired if necessary and sprinkled with ash. Sowing. - Most of this month's planting is done in the ground. We sow arroche in other seedbeds, eggplant in old beds, beets, borage, cardoons, in old beds or in the ground, carrots, white celery up to the 4th, - chervil, curly endive and Rouennaise endive hot, early savoy cabbage, Vertus savoy cabbage, Brussels sprouts, kohlrabi; kohlrabi, cauliflower, broccoli, spring onions, cucumbers and gherkins on old layers; pumpkins, squash, giraumons, watercress and spinach in other seedbeds; broad beans, fennel, beans of all kinds, lettuces, romaine, hot escarole, hot melons, white mustard, turnips, oxalis multiplied by cuttings, parsnips, parsley, chili pepper, salad burnet, leeks, peas, potatoes, purslane, radishes in other seedbeds, periwinkle, salsify, scorzonera, tomatoes, corn. Planting, transplanting and counter-planting. - The previous month's 16. seedlings are transplanted into the nursery. By adding manure, we plant melons on soil and on old layers, we plant them on new trenches with frames and cloches, we plant cucumbers, gherkins, eggplants and cardoons on old carrot layers without turning them over. We transplant beet, white and celeriac in nurseries; we plant Milan des Vertus cabbage, Brussels sprouts and cauliflower as counterplants to chicory, romaine and lettuce. Pumpkins are planted in holes or trenches, while beans are planted in pots with lettuce, romaine and curly endive. Scarole counterplanting takes place in lettuces and romaine: On old beds and in costières, piment̂s and tomatoes are planted. Corn is transplanted. Care for vegetating plants. - When the weather is dry, water abundantly in the morning and evening, preferably during daylight hours. Great care should be taken when removing plants from among the others. All new plantings should be wetted first, then watered for a few days. Planting plots should be well mulched and wet before planting. Young plants should be watered sparingly. Only water plants that are three-quarters mature should be watered abundantly. Never wet romaine, lettuce, spinach, chicory or escarole in the sun, as this heats up the plants and exposes them to rot. The strongest under-frame melons are bathed until the fruit has stopped. Cucumbers and gherkins need very little water during harvesting, and only need to be basked. Melons are pruned morning and evening, which is the most favorable time, as are cucumbers, eggplants and tomatoes. Tomatoes are potted. As in the previous month, care must be taken with planting under glass. While the sun is shining, the windows are covered with a light mulch or Spanish white washed down with water, and frequent weeding and raking are carried out in plantations where there are no seedlings. A weed-free garden is every gardener's badge of excellence. Harvesting. - This is the best time to harvest. Here's an approximate list: Asparagus, artichokes, belles-dames, eggplants, borage, carrots, chervil, cellar and quarry mushrooms, chicory, kale, cauliflower, spring onions, civet, cucumbers, watercress, spinach, tarragon, broad beans, strawberries, spinach, tarragon, broad beans, green beans, lettuces, romaine, melons, white mustard, turnips, white onions, parsnips, parsley, burnet, leeks, peas, pears, parsnips, parsnip, red and pink radishes, rhubarb, savory, thyme, strawberries. Special remarks. - Tomato beds can be replaced by cloaked melons, as can carrots. Tomatoes in cloches can be replaced by cucumbers and eggplants, cauliflowers by melons in trenches, and tomatoes in frames by beans. Melons would be replaced by tomatoes, seed radishes by carrots and seed onions, spring onions by apple cabbage, apple cabbage by carrots, romaine or lettuce, white onions by chicory or cauliflower. The romaines would receive lettuces, chervil, civet, spring onions and leeks. The costières would receive cauliflower, beans or marjoram potatoes. IX Work in June. Glass is becoming less useful. From the 15th, the frames covering the already large melons are removed. As the melons are sold, the frames are transferred to melons with bells that have been prepared for this crop. Frames that are not intended for this purpose are taken into the shed and placed in stacks of fifty. Care should be taken to leave a space between the rows for any repairs that need to be made. As for the bells, don't leave them three inside each other, as is the bad habit when removing them. It's better to prepare a stretcher and stack them six high, separated by a small cleat at the top of each one. They are then transported to the place where they are to be kept. Care should be taken to remove the forks at the same time and put them under cover, and the same precaution should be taken for the cleats to give air to the frames: there is no small saving. The trunks remain on the ground until July. The empty cabbage and onion beds are ploughed, and smoked if necessary, but it's best to have taken this precaution in spring or autumn. We finish turning over the cloches for the last melons. Sowing. - All this month's seedlings are sown in the open ground, but it's safer to sow on an average turned-over bed. Seedlings include : arrowroot, carrots, chervil, chicory, escarole, savoy, Vertus, Brussels, cabbage, kohlrabi, kohlrabi kohlrabi, cauliflower up to the 10th, spring onions, watercress, perennial watercress, spinach, green and dry beans, lettuces, romaine, white mustard, turnips, sorrel, parsnips, leeks, peas, purslane, pink and red radishes in other seedlings, black radishes, raiponce, rhubarb. Planting and disadvantages. - In the first days of the month, we finish planting melons on old layers turned over from cucumbers and gherkins, we plant eggplants and long pepper on old layers and in costières, we do the same for cardoons. White celery is counter-planted in chicory, lettuce and romaine, celeriac in carrots and escarole, and chicory in land emptied by apple cabbage and onions. Milan, Brussels and broccoli sprouts are planted. We continue to counterplant cauliflower with melons, romaine, lettuces, chicory and escarole. Finally, lettuces, romaine, leeks and the last tomatoes are planted. Care for growing crops. - Melons should be pruned and drenched morning and evening if the weather is dry. All plants at three-quarters of their growth need plenty of water. Tomatoes await pruning and trellising, which should not be neglected; the same applies to cucumbers and eggplants. Gherkins need to be spread out properly. Melons still in frames and cloches require a great deal of care; during the heat of the sun, they must be given plenty of air. All new plantings require a lot of water, and others need frequent watering, especially when the sun is hot. Weed frequently, cover cauliflower heads with healthy leaves torn from the mother plant, mulch plants intended for planting, prune and pick pumpkins. Harvesting. - These consist of field asparagus, arugula, eggplant, basil, borage, nasturtium, carrot, cerfiul, quarry mushroom, chicory, escarole, apple cabbage, cauliflower, spring onion, civet, cucumber, garden cress, perennial cress, watercress, spinach, tarragon, broad bean, strawberries, green beans, lettuces, romaine, melons, white mustard, turnips, white onions, sorrel, parsnips, potatoes, stonewort, parsley, salad burnet, pepper, pear, pear, peas, potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes, rhubarb, savory, tetragona, tomatoes, bulbous chervil. Special remarks. - Tomatoes can be replaced by long pepper squares; gherkins by melons, eggplants and cucumbers; cauliflowers and carrots by melon squares. Tomatoes can be replaced by chicory and melons by tomatoes and beans; cabbages by celeriac, spring onions by escarole, seedlings by long pepper, chicory and romaine by carrots, leeks by escarole, chico18 rées or cauliflower, and tomatoes, eggplants or gherkins can be planted in the costières. 8 Work in July. All equipment must be stored in the shed, the frames stacked as previously mentioned, the bins packed and covered with old straw mats or litter. Those that are broken and can be repaired are set aside. All broken glass is collected in a barrel and sold when out of use. Only glass used to make pins should be kept. All trunks must be removed; those on foot are stacked outside. It would be a good idea to cover them with litter or canvas intended for this purpose; those that can be dismantled are put under the shed piece by piece, or in piles outside, with the same precautions as for the former. S 2 :ag A FIFIEFIZI time to make repairs or set aside those that need them so that they can be done as soon as possible. If there's time, it's a good idea to scrape the frames before painting them, remasking them, and replacing broken windows, as it's useful to be ahead on these jobs. Loaders, stretchers and other tools and utensils not currently needed should also be inspected, repaired and sheltered. Empty plots of Bonneuil cabbage and cauliflower are ploughed. If they are to be smoked, only very good manure should be used, the clods should be broken up well, and even moistened if necessary before ploughing. If there is too much drought, all the trouble caused by this can be regained. Ploughing should be done early in the morning, so that you can rest during the heat of the day. Sowing. - You can sow arroche in other plants or crops, carrots, chervil, curly endive, escarole, spring onion, watercress, spinach in other plants; lettuces, romaine, white mustard, turnips, parsnips, leeks, peas, purslane, pink and red radishes in other plants, or on their own, black radishes, raipons. Planting and disadvantages. - The last cardoons are planted, white celery is counter-planted with chicory, lettuce and romaine, and the last celeriac is counter-planted with melons in trenches and potting soil. Curly endive and escarole are planted, cauliflowers are counter-planted in trenches and salads, and melons, gherkins and cucumbers are grown on soil. Milan, Vertus and other large-ribbed cabbages, kohlrabi and turnip cabbages, broccoli, strawberry plants transplanted in May and leeks can all be planted in the ground at this time. Care for growing crops. - Finish pruning melons, cucumbers and pumpkins. Depending on how dry the atmosphere is, they are wetted. Weeds are destroyed, caterpillars attacking all varieties of cabbage are hunted down; the ground is covered with growing plants, and watering is the main care they require. Cucurbits need a lot of water; pumpkins, squash and giraumonts are pruned and pickled. Tomato pruning and trellising is one of the month's major occupations. Harvesting. - Arrochokes, artichokes, basil, borage, nasturtiums, carrots, chervil, quarry mushrooms, curly endive, escarole, Bonneuil cabbage, ordinary Milan cabbage, cauliflower, spring onion, civet, cucumbers, gherkins, watercress, watercress, shallots, spinach, tarragon, broad beans, bulbous chervil, lettuces, romaine, melons, white mustard, turnips, white onions, parsnips, potatoes, stonewort, parsley, long and square peppers, salad burnet, leeks, periwinkle, peas, potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes, tetragona, strawberries, spinach, tomatoes. Seeds are starting to be harvested, so now is the time to look after seed carriers. Special observations. - Tomato beds will be replaced by eggplants; melons will be replaced by gherkins, cucumbers or white celery; tomatoes will replace key melons; escarole, cauliflower and carrots. Melons would give way to cauliflower and carder-pear; chicory and romaine, to carrots and leeks; civet, to pear; seed radishes, to chervil; seedlings, to tomatoes; chicory and white celery, to Bonneuil and red cabbages; escarole and chicory, to toma es; costières would receive chicory, escarole and carrots. XI Work in August. Repairs to frames and bells continue. The land is largely occupied, so there is little plowing to be done. The drought requires some wetting, but this is nothing compared to the previous month. We empty the melon trenches as soon as the fruit is sold, if we don't counter-plant in them. Sowing. - Sowing is infrequent. They are limited to arrowroot, chervil, spring onion, spinach, lettuce, romaine and lamb's lettuce from the 15th; turnips, leeks and late peas, which you can't be sure of harvesting; pink and red radishes, and white onions in nurseries, from the 10th. Planting and disadvantages. - White celery is still planted with radish and spinach seedlings. We counter-plant chicory and escarole, lettuce, romaine and cauliflower. Leeks and watercress are planted with cuttings; escarole and chicory are transplanted with plugs in September. Care for growing crops. - Watering continues; in fact, it is very necessary, as the soil has suffered from the drought. Melon pruning is nearing completion, with only one fruit left. Tomato trellising and pruning require great care. Weeding must be repeated. Plenty of water must be applied when planting, and young plants must be supported by frequent basking. Pumpkins and cauliflowers need to be pruned and wetted every other day, and caterpillars need to be kept at bay. Harvesting. - Arrochokes, artichokes, eggplants, basil, borage, baselle, nasturtiums, carrots, white celery, chervil, bulbous chervil, quarry mushrooms, curly endive, escarole, Bonneuil cabbage, red cabbage, ordinary Milan cabbage, spring onions, pumpkins, cucumbers, gherkins, watercress, perennial and watercress, shallots, garlic, tarragon, broad beans, green and shelled beans, lettuces, romaine, corn, melons, white mustard, turnips, red and yellow onions, sorrel, burnet, leeks, peas, potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes, tomatoes, thyme. - Most seeds are harvested during this month. Special observations. - Tomato, melon and gherkin beds will contain cardoons and cauliflowers; 8 5. P. 297. 3 S aco 29 A escarole and romaine will be replaced by celeriac and the latter by carrots. - Tomatoes will be replaced by chicory and melons by white celery; cauliflowers by melons, seeded spring onions by chervil, seeded radishes by spring onions, burnet or cress, white celery by red and Bonneuil cabbage, escarole and leeks, the latter by chicory, chervil or lamb's lettuce. Chili will be replaced by tomatoes, white celery by escarole, chicory and cauliflower, the latter by leeks; all costières will contain only white celery, escarole or chicory. XII Work in September. We are finishing repairs to the frames, chests and bells, which are due to come into use the following month. There is almost 17. no plowing to be done, as the garden should be fully occupied or nearly so. Sowing. - This is the month when we do the least. We still sow chervil and borage to have them available at all times, bulbous chervil, Rouen chicory and escarole for replanting under frames, apple and beef cabbage for spring, cauliflower, spring onions, spinach and black lettuce in the first few days of the month and later for cold work under frames or cloches, and at the end of the month for hot work. Lamb's lettuce, turnips, pink and red radishes and stone drills are also sown in the early days. Planting and counter-planting. - Almost everything is finished by the previous month. Rouen chicory is transplanted under cloches, to be put under frames the following month at the same time as escarole. We plant strawberry plants from nets and prepare well-smoked ground for planting seedlings to be put under frames over winter. At the end of the month, black lettuces are transplanted cold or placed indoors. Leeks, escarole and lump chicory are planted in empty plots. Start transplanting white onions and sow them in the first few days to keep them in place. Care for growing crops. - Give celery and cauliflower abundant watering, weed frequently and rigorously hunt for caterpillars, cover cauliflower with a leaf from the mother plant, cut down manure to make mushroom wheels, wet the manure, make a floor of it, and reshuffle it. Cover celery to blanch it, stuff the oldest cardoons planted for the same purpose. Harvesting. - Garlic, arrowroot, artichokes, eggplants, borage, nasturtiums, carrots, white celery, celeriac, chervil, bulbous chervil, quarry mushrooms, curly endive, escarole, red cabbage, savoy cabbage, broccoli, cauliflower, spring onions, the last cucumbers and gherkins, pumpkins, watercress, watercress, shallots, spinach, tarragon, broad beans, strawberries, green, shelled and dried beans, lettuces, romaine, lamb's lettuce, melons, white mustard, turnips, red and yellow onions, sorrel, parsnips, potatoes, stonewort, parsley, pepper, salad burnet, leeks, peas, potatoes, purslane, pink and red radishes, tomatoes, thyme. Special observations. - White celery and cabbage can be replaced by carrots and celeriac; escarole by parsley and sorrel; white celery by chicory or the last of the bell melons. Tomatoes would replace celeriac and celery root; lamb's lettuce, chervil; spring onions and civets would be replaced by spinach, radishes, celery and cardoons; escarole by cauliflower, peppers by escarole, the rest of the celery by transplanted onions and lamb's lettuce. In the costières, there would be chicory, escarole, lamb's lettuce and chervil. VEGETABLE GROWING. Under this heading, we understand the cultivation of vegetables that people outside the profession can grow for their own personal use; we will not describe again the methods set out in this book, we refer to the special chapters for details: we will only give here the distribution of the soil and the advice necessary for success. The average family is made up of 5 or 6 people. If this family lives in the country, and if one of its members can devote an hour a day to cultivating a garden of 400 to 500 meters, this family can be sure of having vegetables for its consumption throughout the year, by putting into practice the rules we are about to explain. Similarly, a teacher training college, a seminary, a college, a community of 50 to 60 people will find its vegetable consumption on an area ten times larger, i.e. 4 to 5,000 surface meters. To achieve this, it's absolutely essential to ban all open-air fruit trees from the kitchen garden, and to plant only espaliers. - Cordon or cattail trees should also be banned; only then will you be able to grow good, beautiful vegetables. - Manure and potting soil must be available in sufficient quantities for the area to be cultivated. In the absence of pure horse manure, cow, chicken, rabbit, pig and other types of manure can be used, mixed with sweepings, peelings and weeding residues to form a compost; shrub leaves and any decomposing organic products that may be available can also be added. However, to make the layers, you'll always need a supply of horse manure, a supply of potting soil for spring sowing and planting, and a supply of mulch for summer covering. A vegetable garden for 5 or 6 people requires 7 frames and 50 bells, which, with tools, requires an expenditure of around 200 francs. For a gathering of 50 or 60 people, you'll need 70 frames and 500 bells, the cost of which, combined with the rest of the equipment, comes to around 1,250 francs. In such cases, it's a good idea to adopt the watering system we've described. The cost can be appreciated by the following estimate, made for the model of vegetable garden whose plan we present to our readers: A manege pump........ 1,200 fr. 300 meters of lead pipes. 1,000 42 water outlets or taps.... 15,000-liter tank, 3-millimeter sheet metal....... Tank assembly......... 14 meters of rubber and lance..................... 1 stopcock...........- 500 600 400 160 30 A small steam engine... 2,000 Total................ 5,890 It would be a good idea to obtain 300 meters of potting soil in the first year, at an estimated cost of 1,200 francs, excluding transport costs. It is essential to secure manure for a stable of 15 to 20 horses, which costs an average of 3 francs per month per horse. There's nothing frightening about these figures if you consider that, in a house of this kind, the steam engine and the water from the reservoir can meet needs other than watering the vegetable garden, that the initial costs are quickly reimbursed by the savings resulting from the abundant vegetables harvested, and that the permanent expense is reduced after a short time to an annual sum of 600 to 700 francs for the purchase of manure. With this in mind, we'll now give a table of the plants to be grown for the two potacers we've taken as types, asking the reader to look up the details of their cultivation in chapters V, VI and VII. MSTORY OF MARKET GARDENING, ITS PROGRESS AND IMPROVEMENTS. Until the sixteenth century, we know nothing precise about market gardening; vegetables native to France or long-acclimatized there were grown only on soil or on beds in the open air. 16th century. Rabelais sends the first lettuce and romaine seeds from Rome to Cardinal d'Estrées (1540). 17th century. Work on vegetable plants by Dr. Mizauld. (1605.) First mention of the use of bells by Olivier de Serres. (1623.) Cultivation of melons using rolling boxes retracted into the greenhouse during frost and at night: Olivier de Serres. (1623.) Cultivation of melons on beds and cauliflowers; Théâtre du Jardinier, by Claude Mollet, gardener to Louis XIII. (1643.) Cultivation of early vegetables, use of frames by La Quintinye, head gardener of the Versailles kitchen garden. (1688.) 18th century. Cultivation of pineapples and potatoes using hot greenhouses by Gondoin, gardener at the Château de Choisy-le-Roi under Louis XV. First greengrocers: - Tassère, gardener to the Régent in Bagnolet. - Debille, Ébrard, Valette, François, Fournier, Heude, Jaulin, Marie, market gardeners in Paris, start using frames for industrial cultivation. (1780 to 1800.) Forced strawberry plants, by Legrand (1776). - Hot cultivation of peas, beans and carrots, by Decouflé. (1788.) - Hot cultivation of curly endive, by Stainville, (1791.) - Invention of the watering crank, first use, by Bourgeois, market gardener in Grenelle. (1792.) Heated white asparagus, by Quentin. (1792.) Green asparagus, by Marie. (1800.) 19th century. Hot cauliflower, by Besnard. (1811.) Cold Romaines under cloches: Dulac, Chemin, Fanfan Quentin. (1812.) Founding of the Horticultural Society. (1827.) Adoption of the fulling pump. (1836.) Adoption of lance watering, by 1. Ponce (1859. END. PagesPREFACE..............................................
